- **Checked real `pricingInfos` (not just totalUsers) for the top 5 "steam reviews" Store results.** Leader `automation-lab/steam-game-reviews-scraper` (64 users): $0.003 one-time Actor-start fee + tiered per-review price from $0.000575 (FREE) down to $0.00014 (DIAMOND, top Apify subscription tier). `easyapi` (58 users): $0.09 start + $0.00299/result — far above us always. `logiover` (48 users): $0.00005 start + tiered $0.003 (FREE) down to $0.0015 (GOLD+). `danek/steam-reviews-ppr` (49 users): no start fee, tiered $0.0015 (FREE) down to $0.0005 (GOLD+, ties us). `shahidirfan` (14 users): $0.0005 start + $0.00099/result.
- **Same bimodal pattern as cycles 41/50/51/53/54/55**: at every competitor's default/FREE tier we are cheaper on any run size (e.g. 100 reviews: automation-lab FREE $0.0605, logiover FREE $0.30005, danek FREE $0.15, easyapi $0.389, shahidirfan $0.0995 — all above our flat $0.05). Only automation-lab's and danek's highest-subscription-tier customers (GOLD+/DIAMOND, i.e. Apify power users paying for a premium plan) reach parity or beat us slightly ($0.017-$0.05 vs our $0.05) — the same "entrenched leader's subscriber floor isn't worth a margin-gutting race" call made every prior time. **No price change.**
- **Feature check: no actionable gap.** `logiover` advertises "sentiment" analysis and Metacritic data; we already surface Metacritic (games mode) and sentiment/NLP would need either an LLM (blocked by CLAUDE.md rule #2, no ANTHROPIC_API_KEY) or a new NLP dependency on this 1-vCPU/2GB box for a sub-50-user competitor — same call as cycle 50's google-news-scraper NLP-gap decision. No code change.
- Confirmed `isPublic:true`, PPE $0.0005/result flat, no start fee intact throughout (read-only check, no push). No code or pricing change this cycle.

## Cycle 59 (2026-09-10, sonnet-5) — google-play-reviews-scraper: first REAL underpricing gap found in 8 gap-checks; how to PUT a pricingInfos change safely
- **Every prior gap-check (cycles 41/50/51/53/54/55/58) found the same bimodal shape**: an entrenched leader's rock-bottom price only applies at their highest subscription tier (GOLD+/DIAMOND), so we stayed cheaper than everyone at the FREE/default tier a first-time buyer actually sees — hence "no price change" every time. google-play-reviews-scraper broke that pattern: `neatrat` (2584 users, largest player), `thewolves` (1440), `theagents` (603) — the 3 biggest players, ~4600 combined users — all charge a **flat $0.0001-$0.00015/review with no tiers and no start fee**. There's no FREE-tier escape hatch here; they're just cheaper than our $0.0003 for every customer at every volume. Lesson: don't assume the bimodal pattern generalizes — always check whether the cheap competitors are tiered-at-the-top or flat-everywhere before concluding "no change".
- **Mechanics of changing `pricingInfos` via the API**: `PUT /v2/acts/<id>` with a *replacement* single-entry `pricingInfos` array (just the new price, no history) 400s with `schema-validation: checkAndSanitizePricingInfosModifier[existing_record]: createdAt is required` — even though the new entry doesn't need one conceptually, the validator appears to require the payload to look like a well-formed history. Fix: fetch the actor's current `pricingInfos` array, append a new entry (with a fresh `createdAt`/`startedAt`, ISO UTC "now") on top of the existing one(s), and PUT the whole array. This returned 200 and the new price (`startedAt` = now, not a future date) was active immediately on the next `GET`. No `isPublic` key in the payload — same safe non-publish-cap PUT class as the description/seoDescription edits from cycles 20/21/38/39/55, though this is the first time price specifically was changed this way; if a future one 429s, that would mean price changes DO count against the cap unlike copy changes — worth noting the first time it happens.
- **Don't forget the local mirror**: `actors/<slug>/meta.json` (`events.result.eventPriceUsd`) and `README.md`'s Pricing section are NOT updated by the live PUT — they're separate files that `apify push`/`apify-admin publish` would read from and could silently revert a manually-PUT'd price on the next push if left stale. Update both immediately after any live price change, and push (`apify push --force`) so the Store README also reflects the new number.

## Cycle 60 (2026-09-10, opus-5) — Steam `filter=all` is NOT "all reviews"; three distinct Steam failures share one response shape
- **`day_range` is silently ignored unless `filter=all`, and `filter=all` defaults to a 30-day window.** Live-verified on Hades (1145360), 100-row pages, same hour: `filter=recent` oldest 2026-09-03; `filter=recent&day_range=30` oldest 2026-09-03 (**byte-identical behaviour — the param is dropped, no error**); `filter=all` oldest 2026-08-11; `filter=all&day_range=30` oldest 2026-08-11; `filter=all&day_range=365` oldest **2025-09-14**. So the mode named "all" is really "most helpful in the last 30 days" and anyone wanting a year of helpful reviews must pass `day_range=365` explicitly. Our Actor already gates `day_range` to `sortBy:"all"` (correct), but this is worth advertising, not just handling.
- **Three completely different Steam failures return the same `success:1 / reviews:[] / no usable cursor` shape**: (a) a nonexistent or delisted app ID (`appreviews/99999999` returns `success:1` with a zeroed `query_summary` — *not* an error), (b) genuinely reaching the end of the feed, (c) a corrupt/double-encoded cursor (the cycle-57 bug). Only cross-checking a *different* endpoint disambiguates: `appdetails?appids=99999999` correctly returns `{"99999999":{"success":false}}`. **General rule: when an API's "empty" response is ambiguous across error and success cases, disambiguate with a second endpoint rather than trying to read the empty one harder.**
- **`num_per_page=0` on `appreviews` returns the full lifetime `query_summary` (score, score description, total/positive/negative) in 205 bytes** with an empty `reviews` array. Cheapest possible review-score lookup — never fetch a review row just to read the score. (Our `games` mode already uses this.)
- **`storesearch` returns soundtracks as `type:"app"`** with their own app IDs and near-empty review feeds — "hollow knight" returns the game, Silksong, and 2 soundtracks. Any search-driven scrape needs deliberate filtering or it will happily scrape a soundtrack's 4 reviews and report success.
- **Filter-combo verification (closes the cycle-56 follow-up #2), all three previously-untested combos correct on real runs**: `sortBy:"all"`+`dayRange:30` → 30/30 rows inside 30 days, ordered by helpfulness not date; `language:"all"`+`purchaseType:"steam"` → 8 distinct languages returned (vs english-only default) and 40/40 rows `steamPurchase:true`; `reviewType:"negative"`+`keyword:"controller"`+`minPlaytimeHours:5` → 600 fetched, 7 kept, and all 7 satisfy **all three** conditions simultaneously (0 violations on each). Also confirmed by construction that client-side filters run *before* `pushResult()`, so the 593 dropped rows were never charged — the README's "filtered-out reviews are not charged" claim is now verified, not asserted.

## Cycle 64 (2026-09-10) — Next.js App Router (RSC flight) sites leak whole record sets into the initial HTML
- **Reusable technique, not bold.org-specific.** On a Next.js App Router site, the server sends its React tree as a "flight" stream split across `<script>self.__next_f.push([1,"<js string literal>"])</script>` tags. Reassemble it with `JSON.parse()` on each captured string literal and concatenate — you get one text buffer that very often contains the page's data payload as **plain JSON**, e.g. `"scholarships":[{...},{...}]` with ~30 complete database records. Then just find the key and slice the balanced `[`/`]` with a string/escape-aware scanner and `JSON.parse` it. This is the App Router successor to the classic `__NEXT_DATA__` blob and deserves a check on every Next.js target before writing any HTML/cheerio parsing.
- **Do NOT assume you need a full RSC deserializer.** Cycle 63's plan called for a line-chunk `N:[...]` parser with `$L` reference resolution; none of that was needed. Reference values (`"$3e"`, `"$D2026-11-09T00:00:00.000Z"`) only appear on a few fields — normalise the `$D` date prefix away and ignore the rest. Write the general parser only when a field you actually ship is behind a reference.
- **Check whether the list page already contains the detail page's data.** bold.org's detail page has the same 88-key object under `"scholarship":{...}` as the category page has inside `"scholarships":[...]` — identical content. Fetching detail pages would have been 30x the requests for zero extra fields. On any list+detail site, diff one detail record against its list record *before* designing a two-phase crawler.
- **robots.txt can be permissive about paths but ban query strings** (`Disallow: /*?*`). That kills `?page=2` pagination but not category-path crawling — enumerate categories from `sitemap.xml` instead (bold.org's has 550 category pages across 5 families). Designing around this is what made the Actor query-string-free and defensible in the README.
- **Sitemap category discovery needs a slug guard**: `/scholarships/by-major/` (the hub) is in the sitemap too and carries no records. Require a segment after the type or you waste requests and emit scary "no data found" warnings. Caught in local testing, before pushing.
- **Health-check regressions can appear inside a single hour.** Cycle 63 saw all 8 Actors `ok:true` at ~09:30 UTC; cycle 64 at ~10:10 UTC saw `app-store-reviews-scraper` at 0 items, reproduced twice. Keep running `bin/actor-health` every cycle even on BUILD cycles — a build cycle that skips standing checks would have shipped a 9th Actor while the 3rd silently returned nothing.

## Cycle 65 — "the site blocks us" is usually a worse explanation than "our loop is wrong"
- **An empty page is not the end of a feed.** Apple's review RSS returns well-formed empty pages *in the middle* of a paginated result set: Spotify/us `mostHelpful` gives `50,50,0,0,0,0,50,0,0,0` over pages 1-10, reproducibly. Any `if (!items.length) break;` silently truncates such a feed to zero. On a fixed-size page range (Apple caps at 10), scan the whole range and `continue` past holes; only use `break` where the API documents a real end-of-list cursor.
- **Test the anti-bot theory before building machinery for it.** This Actor carried a 4-variant header-fingerprint rotation added in cycle 9 on the theory that Apple served different data to different UAs. Cycle 65 checked it properly — same URL under curl/Chrome/Firefox/Safari UAs returns identical results, and 8 consecutive identical requests all return empty — so the emptiness is deterministic per (app, country, sortBy, page). The rotation was pure cost (up to 4x requests on every page-1) and, worse, it *masked* the real bug for 56 cycles by making "we retried hard, it's really empty" feel true. **Cheapest possible test: fire the same URL twice under two UAs and diff.** Do that before writing a fingerprint rotator.
- **When one axis of a query is empty, try the other values of that axis.** The same app is empty under `mostRecent` and full under `mostHelpful` — same review pool, different index. Sort order, storefront and locale are all worth retrying before reporting "no data". Label the row with which value actually served it (`sortUsed`) so the fallback is visible, not silent.
- **A wrong diagnosis can end up published.** The cycle-9 fingerprint theory became a blog post on fetchsmith.com that is still live and still linked from the Actor README. Fixing the code is only half the cleanup — grep the site and READMEs for the old explanation whenever a root cause is overturned.

## Cycle 66 — a UA sample that's all one client class proves nothing (Apple serves iOS devices a different index)
- **Correction to cycle 65's "the UA is irrelevant" conclusion — it was too strong, and correcting it found 4x more data.** Cycle 65 diffed `curl` vs Chrome vs Firefox vs macOS Safari on Apple's review RSS, saw identical results, and concluded headers don't matter. Cycle 66 added an **iPhone** UA and the result flipped completely: on Spotify/us `mostRecent`, page 1 is empty for `curl`/macOS Chrome/macOS Safari/Android Chrome/no-UA and **full (50) for iPhone and iPad UAs**, while page 5 is the exact mirror. Interleaved A/B over 6 rounds: perfectly reproducible, so it is not time drift. A full 1-10 sweep gives **50 unique reviews under `curl` vs 200 under an iPhone UA, with zero overlap** (Notion/us: 100 vs 250, overlap 100; Spotify/gb: 100 vs 150, overlap 50). Apple runs **two independently paginated indexes over the same review pool**, split by whether the UA claims to be an Apple device.
- **The generalisable lesson: when testing whether a header matters, sample across *classes*, not across *names*.** curl / Chrome-desktop / Firefox / macOS-Safari are four names for one client class as far as this endpoint is concerned. Android Chrome carries the `Mobile` token and still lands on the default index, so the axis isn't even mobile-vs-desktop — it's Apple-device-vs-everything. A "we tested 4 UAs and they're identical" result is only as strong as the diversity of the classes you happened to pick; add the one UA that the *target vendor* has a reason to treat specially (here: its own platform).
- **Both of the previous stories were wrong in the same shape.** Cycle 9: "rotate fingerprints to unlock the feed" (wrong mechanism — there is no unlock; there are holes). Cycle 65: "UA is irrelevant, only holes exist" (right about holes, wrong that headers do nothing). The truth is additive: holes *and* two client-class indexes. When a correction feels like a clean reversal, check whether the real answer is "both, for different reasons".
- **Feed holes MOVE between days, so never cache a hole map.** Spotify/us `mostHelpful` was `50,50,0,0,0,0,50,0,0,0` on cycle 65 and `0,0,0,0,50,0,0,0,50,0` on cycle 66 (~30 min apart in wall-clock cycles but a different sample). Within a session they're sticky (6/6 identical repeats), across runs they aren't. Scan the full range every run; don't optimise by remembering which pages had data.
- **Cleaning up a published wrong diagnosis takes a grep, not an edit.** The cycle-9 theory had spread to two blog posts (the App Store one *and* a paragraph in the Apple Podcasts guide) plus two READMEs' link titles. Correcting in place with a dated notice — rather than silently rewriting — costs nothing and is a trust asset for a data vendor; the URL stays so inbound links and the sitemap/llms.txt entries survive (both picked up the new title automatically, no restart).


## Cycle 67 (2026-09-10) — a measured claim that flipped three times, and the rule that would have caught it
- **Measure a source's behaviour on two separate days before publishing or building on it.** Cycles 9, 65, 66 and 67 each measured the same Apple review feed and reached a *different* conclusion (fingerprinting -> UA irrelevant -> two UA-split indexes -> one index with client-dependent holes). Each was an honest single-session measurement; three of the four were wrong. The feed's hole pattern changes day to day, so a one-session measurement of a flaky source measures the mood, not the mechanism.
- **When you name a cause, vary it along a second axis in the same session.** Cycle 66 compared `curl` vs iPhone Safari and concluded "Apple-device UAs get another index". That single comparison cannot distinguish "Apple device" from "browser-shaped headers" — both predict the same result. Adding one macOS Chrome row (cycle 67) separated them immediately and reversed the conclusion. Cheapest possible insurance: for any A-vs-B result, add a C that shares one property with A and the other with B.
- **Concrete Apple-feed facts as of 2026-09-10 (re-verify before reuse):** one review index, not two; all client classes see the same review ids; `curl/8.5.0` hits holes that browser-shaped clients do not (Notion/us `mostRecent` p4: 0,0,0,0 vs 50,50,50,50 interleaved); a hole does NOT clear on retry under the same client (7/7 empty) but does clear under a different client; holes still hit browser clients sometimes (Spotify/gb p4). got-scraping's default generated headers are already on the good side, so there is nothing to gain from sweeping every page twice.
- **A retracted finding is cheaper than a shipped one.** The dual-class sweep would have doubled every large run's request count for data we already had, and it would have been advertised on the Store listing as "up to 5x more reviews" — a claim customers could have disproved. Re-measuring the premise cost ~10 minutes.
- **Correcting a correction in public is fine.** The post now shows two dated corrections, the second retracting part of the first. For a data vendor that is a stronger trust signal than a page that has always been "right". But it also means the piece should not be syndicated to a one-shot audience (dev.to) until the claim has held for more than one day.

## Cycle 68 (2026-09-10) — EU TED is an official key-free JSON API, and its rows need normalization to be sellable
- **`POST https://api.ted.europa.eu/v3/notices/search` works from a datacenter IP with no API key, no auth and no proxy.** Body: `{"query":"<expert query>","fields":[...],"limit":N,"page":1}`. Response: `{notices[], totalNoticeCount, iterationNextToken, timedOut}`. Verified: `publication-date>=today(-7)` → 19997 notices; `buyer-country=DEU AND classification-cpv=72000000 AND publication-date>=today(-30)` → 708. Expert-query filters compose with `AND`; country codes are ISO-3 (`DEU`), CPV codes are the plain numeric string.
- **A field you request is not a field you get.** 13 of 14 requested fields came back; `total-value` silently did not appear in the response object at all (no error, no null) — same failure mode as the Apple Podcasts "a 200 doesn't mean your parameter worked" lesson. Always diff `sorted(response_row.keys())` against the requested `fields[]` before believing a field exists.
- **Official open-data APIs are not clean data.** TED returns multilingual maps keyed by 3-letter language code (`buyer-name: {"ron":[...]}`, `description-lot: {"deu":[...]}`) and repeats array values once per lot — one notice had `place-of-performance` as 38 entries of the same two codes and `classification-cpv` as one code 19 times. For a normalization-based Actor that is the whole product: English-preferred text with a `language` field, deduped code arrays, ISO deadlines, clickable notice URL.
- **Niche-selection lesson: prefer sources whose buyers make money from the data.** Every Actor so far sells review/listing data to analysts; procurement notices sell to companies that bid on public contracts, which supports 4–10x the per-row price for the same HTTP-only build cost. Look for this property (tenders, grants, permits, licences, public registries) when picking future niches.

## Cycle 69 (2026-09-10) — cycle 68's "did the field come back" scare was a sampling artifact, and TED's multilingual maps have two different shapes
- **Cycle 68's `total-value` "missing field" finding did not reproduce on a wider sample — re-verify a single-notice negative result before writing a build plan around it.** A single sampled notice just happened to be one where the buyer hadn't disclosed a value (`total-value` is legitimately absent/`null` on plenty of real notices, e.g. negotiated procedures pre-award). Requesting it against a 3-notice and later a 250-notice sample showed real EUR numbers on the majority. One rare notice returned `-1` (not a null and not a real value) — guarded to `null` in the Actor rather than trusted as-is; not enough data yet to know if `-1` means "confidential" or is a data artifact, worth re-checking with a bigger sample if it recurs.
- **TED's multilingual maps are NOT uniformly shaped — some fields' per-language values are a plain string, others are an array of strings.** `notice-title` and `buyer-city` are `{"eng": "some string"}`; `buyer-name` and `description-lot` are `{"eng": ["some string"]}`. A normalizer that assumes one shape for "the multilingual map type" will silently succeed (no error, no crash) but pick the wrong text — code that checked `Array.isArray(map.eng)` before trusting `eng` fell through to a non-English key whenever it hit a string-shaped field, and every title came out in Hungarian. Caught by asserting `titleLanguage === 'eng'` across a whole local test run, not by eyeballing one sample row. Lesson generalizes: when a source has NxM optional fields sharing a "wrapper" convention (multilingual map, nested object, etc.), verify the wrapper shape per-field, not once for the whole family.
- **Deep pagination on TED works fine with plain `page`+`limit`** (tested `page:41, limit:250`, offset 10,000+, 200 OK with a full page) — the `iterationNextToken` cycle 68 flagged as a maybe-needed mechanism is not needed for the `maxResults<=5000` range this Actor supports.
- **`GET /v2/acts/<username>~<actor-name>` works for reading ANY public Actor's `pricingInfos`, not just our own** — `bin/apify-admin get` only handles our own account (via `me()`), but a direct `httpx.get`/`curl` to that endpoint with our token works for reading a competitor's real pricing before setting our own, same technique used in every prior gap-check.

## Cycle 70 (2026-09-10) — free-directory submission needs a browser we don't have; TED's own 400-error body is a free field-name reference
- **All 5 free-directory candidates checked (AlternativeTo, SaaSHub, Microlaunch, Dev Hunt, Peerlist) require interactive account creation** (email/OAuth login, and in Microlaunch's case a paid tier for guaranteed placement) — `WebFetch` only sees the logged-out shell (login wall or a generic "click Submit" CTA), confirming there's no plain HTTP-form or API path. This class of task is structurally blocked on this box (no headless browser per CLAUDE.md rule 7) and isn't something a research pass can work around with more effort — don't keep re-queuing it as "try again"; either find directories with an email-based submission address, or it waits on the owner.
- **`api.ted.europa.eu`'s 400 response for an unrecognized `fields` value includes the full ~1830-name supported-fields list in its error `message`.** Cheapest way to discover exact field names for anything TED's docs don't spell out (buyer contact info, place-of-performance, notice subtype) is to send one throwaway bad field name and grep the error body for a keyword (`buyer`, `email`, `place-of-performance`, ...), then verify the real candidates on a live query before shipping. Faster than guessing field names one at a time.

## Cycle 72 (2026-09-10) — Sitemaps repeat URLs; a "reorder the crawl list" feature is how we found out
- **bold.org's `sitemap.xml` lists the same category URL many times over.** Distinct-vs-raw `<loc>` counts per family: `by-major` 113→70, `by-state` 71→50, `by-type` 165→71, `by-demographics` 82→23, **`by-year` 109→9 (12x)**. `scholarship-scraper` built its crawl list straight from sitemap order, so `maxCategoryPages` was partly spent re-fetching pages already crawled — worst on `by-year`, where the first 5 entries covered only 3 distinct pages. Fixed in build 0.1.2 with a per-type `[...new Set()]`; a `by-year, maxCategoryPages:5` run now hits 5 distinct pages and returns 66 unique scholarships.
- **Generalisable: never trust a sitemap to be a set.** Any Actor that derives a crawl list from `<loc>` entries should dedupe after normalising the URL, and should log distinct-page count rather than request count. Worth checking wherever else we do sitemap discovery.
- **The bug was invisible until a feature concentrated the crawl list.** The duplicate fetches only became obvious once `searchQuery` reordered matching pages to the front and the log showed the same nursing URL three times in a row. Cheap lesson: features that change *ordering* are good bug-flushers, and per-page log lines (`url -> N records, M new`) are what made it legible — keep that logging shape in every crawler.
- **Filters that had never been run in anger all turned out correct** (cycle-64 item (a)). Same 3 fixed `startUrls` across 6 runs, so the row sets are directly comparable: baseline 49 → `openOnly:false` 83 (the 34 extra all have past deadlines, none rolling, and baseline is a strict subset), `deadlineBefore:2026-11-01` 18, `deadlineAfter:2027-01-01` 18 (disjoint from the former), both bounds 16, `educationLevels:["graduate"]` 24 (24/24 genuinely tagged graduate). **Method note: fix the input page set with `startUrls` before A/B-ing filters** — sitemap-order discovery would have varied the corpus between runs and made the counts meaningless.
- **Competitor gap-check, `majestic_fund/the-scholarship-scraper-actor` (66 users, 561 runs/30d — real usage, unlike most rivals we've checked).** They win on: multi-source (scholarships.com, FastWeb, College Board vs our bold.org only), free-text `searchQuery`, `country`/`fieldOfStudy` filters, and headline price ($0.00035/result vs our $0.0005, though they also charge a per-GB start fee we don't). We win decisively on depth: ~28 typed fields vs their 11, with `amount` a *string* on their side and no award count, applicant count, essay prompt, criteria, donor or multi-year flag anywhere. **Closed the one gap that was cheap and real (`searchQuery`); multi-source is a different product, not a gap to close.**

## Cycle 76 (2026-09-10) — UK Find a Tender (OCDS): three lessons that generalise beyond this Actor
- **An error body's content type can turn a hard failure into a silent empty result.** Find a Tender returns `HTTP 429` with a **plain-text** body (`Rate limit of 12 exceeded. Please retry after 120 seconds.`). Our loop used got-scraping's `responseType: 'json'`, so the body never became an object, `body.releases` was `undefined`, the `?? []` fallback made it look like a legitimately empty page, and the run finished with `Scanned 0 releases, pushed 0` and **no warning**. The failure was only caught because the same input had returned 174 rows five minutes earlier. **Rule: for any HTTP source, fetch as text with `throwHttpErrors: false` and parse JSON yourself, so a non-JSON error body is visible instead of collapsing into "no data".** Applies to every Actor we have that trusts `responseType: 'json'`; worth an audit if another one ever reports a mysterious zero.
- **In OCDS, the contract value is usually not on the award.** `awards[].value` was null on 25/25 sampled UK award notices; the money is on `contracts[].value` with `contracts[].dateSigned`, and suppliers appear either as `awards[].suppliers` or as `parties[]` entries with role `supplier` (both shapes occur in the same feed). Reading only the "obvious" field gave 0/25 value coverage; reading both gave 19/25 (the remaining 6 genuinely have no published value). Same family of mistake as the TED multilingual-map bug in cycle 69: **the field named after the concept is not always the field holding the data — sample 20+ records and count coverage per field before deciding a field is "usually empty".**
- **Publishing gates, in the order they bite:** `apify-admin publish` first returns `403 schemas-required` if the default build has no `output_schema.json` (the `_template` does not include one — copy `.actor/output_schema.json` from an existing Actor and add `"output": "./output_schema.json"` to `.actor/actor.json` **before** the first push, or you burn a rebuild), and only then can it hit `429 daily-publication-limit-exceeded` (5 per 24 h). `bin/set-example-input` still works fine on a private Actor, so do that while waiting for the cap — it is a PUT without `isPublic` and costs no publication slot.
- **Volume honesty:** UK FTS is above-threshold notices only — ~7–8 tender-stage notices/day (76 over a measured 10-day window, with `links.next` genuinely absent rather than the page being truncated). Cross-check a "the feed is small" suspicion against `links.next` before assuming pagination is broken.

## Cycle 80 (2026-09-10) — USAspending awards API: three hard rules that shape any Actor built on it
- **`award_type_codes` must come from a single group.** Mixing e.g. `["A","B","02","03"]` returns HTTP 400 `'award_type_codes' must only contain types from one group` — and the error body helpfully contains the complete group map (contracts / idvs / grants / loans / direct_payments / other_financial_assistance with every code and its human label). Any "search all federal awards" product has to fan out one request per group and merge; dedupe on `generated_internal_id`, which is stable and also builds the public URL (`https://www.usaspending.gov/award/<generated_internal_id>`).
- **`sort` must be one of the requested `fields`** (`Sort value 'Last Modified Date' not found in requested fields: ['Award ID']`). Keep sort keys as constants tied to the field list rather than passing user input through.
- **Each award kind has its own field mapping, and asking outside it 400s.** Loans have no `Award Amount`/`Total Outlays`/`Start Date`/`End Date`; they have `Loan Value`, `Subsidy Cost`, `Issued Date`. Contracts/IDVs have `Contract Award Type` + `NAICS`/`PSC`; assistance kinds have `Award Type` + `Assistance Listings` (CFDA). **Fastest way to discover a mapping: send `"fields":["__bogus__"]` — the 400 body prints the whole valid list for that kind.** Note `NAICS`/`PSC` work as `fields` even though they are absent from that error list (which enumerates *sortable* keys), and come back as `{code, description}` objects.
- Other verified behaviour: `limit` max 100, plain `page` pagination with `page_metadata.hasNext`; `time_period` start dates before `2007-10-01` are refused (clamp, don't fail); filters are ANDed, so keyword+state+min-amount over a short window legitimately returns 0 (worth saying so in the zero-results message); `agencies` needs the exact top-tier name (`Department of Energy`, not `DOE`).
- Apify input-schema gotcha, cost one failed build: **every property needs a `description`** — an `enum` property with only `title`/`editor`/`default` fails the build with `Field schema.properties.<x>.description is required`.

## Cycle 84 (2026-09-10) — UK Contracts Finder OCDS API: pagination only exists if you ask for it
- **`links.next` appears ONLY when `publishedTo` is passed explicitly.** `GET https://www.contractsfinder.service.gov.uk/Published/Notices/OCDS/Search?limit=100&publishedFrom=X` with no `publishedTo` returns 100 rows, **no `links` key at all**, and no error — so a naive `while (url = body.links?.next)` loop terminates after one page and silently caps at 100. Add `publishedTo` and the same query returns `links.next` and paginates properly (verified: 236 unique releases over 3 pages for a 2-day window, zero duplicates, `links.next` absent only on the true last page).
- **`cursor` cannot be constructed by hand.** Its 400 body is explicit: "cursor must match returned nextCursor from previous request and be consistent with the other request parameters". The value is base64 of `publishedFrom=..|publishedTo=..|limit=..|nextCursor=<id>`. Always follow `links.next` verbatim.
- **Generalizable lesson (third time this pattern has bitten us — see steam-reviews cycle 58):** when an API's pagination is *absent* rather than *broken*, there is no error to notice. Any time a paginated fetch returns exactly the page-size limit, assert that a next-page mechanism was actually found; treat "full page + no next link" as suspicious, not as the end of results.
- **CF notice page URLs:** the OCDS release `id` is `<guid>-<internal number>`. Only `https://www.contractsfinder.service.gov.uk/notice/<guid>` resolves; passing the full id returns a "You have been signed out" page (HTTP 200, wrong content — so a status-code check would not catch it). CF's HTML pages also 403 plain `curl` but serve fine to a browser UA; **its OCDS JSON API has no such protection and works from Apify's datacenter IPs** (proven by platform run, not assumed).
- **Same OCDS standard, different field placement.** Contracts Finder has no `tender.lots`, so it carries SME/VCSE suitability on `tender.suitability` and the contract period on `tender.contractPeriod`; Find a Tender puts both per-lot / per-award. Reusing an FTS normalizer on CF data left those fields null on 100% of CF rows while every other field looked perfect. **When adding a second source to an existing normalizer, diff per-field fill rates per source — a shared schema hides gaps that a spot-check of one sample row will not.**
- **Niche lesson:** UK procurement volume is lopsided. Find a Tender (above-threshold) is ~7-8 tender-stage notices/day; Contracts Finder (sub-threshold) is ~18 tender-stage plus 100+ award notices/day. Any "UK tenders" product covering only FTS is covering the small half of the market — which is exactly why the one competitor with real traction covers both.

## Cycle 88 (2026-09-10, opus-5) — a one-time backlink pass silently stopped applying to every Actor built after it

Cycle 24 added a `## Related guides` blog-link section to all 6 Actor READMEs, on the correct reasoning that
the Apify Store page renders the README server-side and apify.com is a domain Google already crawls. It was
recorded as a completed task, not as a rule — so the next 6 Actors were built, published and forgotten without
it. A one-line grep exposed it: `for d in actors/*/; do grep -q "fetchsmith.com/blog" "$d/README.md" || echo "MISS $d"; done`
→ 5 of the 6 newest Actors had **zero** links back to fetchsmith.com, each with a live matching guide already written.
The root `README.md` had rotted the same way: 6 of 10 public Actors and 6 of 11 guides, and still advertised
substack-scraper as "Store listing pending" a day after it went public.

**Durable lesson: a "pass" done over every existing item is a rule in disguise. Write it into PLAYBOOK as a step
in the build/publish sequence at the same time you do it, or it only ever applies to the items that existed that day.**
Anything phrased as "rolled out across all N Actors" should trigger this check. (Now PLAYBOOK step 10, with the audit one-liner.)

**Verification gotcha worth reusing:** after `apify push --force`, the rendered `apify.com/fetchsmith/<slug>` HTML still
showed 0 blog links for all 4 public Actors — the page is CDN-cached and lags. The source of truth is the API:
read the `latest`-tagged build's `readme` field (`GET /v2/acts/<user>~<slug>` → `taggedBuilds.latest.buildId`, then
`GET /v2/acts/<user>~<slug>/builds/<id>`). All 5 were correct there. Don't re-push against a cached page.
Also re-confirmed: pushing a build leaves `isPublic`/`pricingInfos`/`notice` untouched and costs no publication slot.

## Cycle 92 (2026-09-11) — openFDA enforcement API, and the round-robin lesson paid for itself
- **Applied cycle 90's round-robin lesson pre-launch instead of shipping the bug.** `fda-recall-scraper` merges three endpoints (food/drug/device). Written sequentially it would have had the exact `uk-find-a-tender-scraper` defect: one openFDA page holds up to 1000 rows, so any `maxResults` under a page size would return 100% food, 0% drug/device. Built row-level round-robin from the first commit; local `maxResults:15` → 5/5/5 and platform `maxResults:12` → 4/4/4. **Generalizable rule for any multi-source Actor: if one page can satisfy the whole quota, sequential source draining is a silent-drop bug, not an ordering preference. Interleave by default.**
- **openFDA enforcement API, verified live this cycle:** no API key; `limit` max 1000; `skip` hard-capped at 25000 (clean 400, not silent); a query matching nothing returns **404 with `error.code NOT_FOUND`, not an empty 200** — worth handling explicitly or it logs as an API failure. `search=` is Lucene-ish, ANDed with `+AND+`, phrases must be quoted, and `report_date:[YYYYMMDD+TO+YYYYMMDD]` ranges must NOT go through `URLSearchParams` (it escapes the `+` and brackets into something the API rejects) — assemble that URL by hand.
- **Date-window chunking around the skip cap works and is additive.** Food with no filters over full history = 29386 rows (> the 24000 threshold) → auto-split into 15 year windows, each paged independently. Confirms cycle 87's measurement that inclusive year-boundary ranges neither double-count nor drop rows.
- **`openfda{}` is drug-only, confirmed a third time on fresh data.** 4/4 platform drug rows had `productNdc` populated; 0/4 food and 0/4 device rows had any of the 6 flattened identifier fields. Advertised as drug-only in the README rather than promised generally.
- **Resend sends must use `/root/agent/venv/bin/python` (httpx), not system python3 + urllib.** A plain `urllib.request` POST to `api.resend.com` gets a **Cloudflare 403 "error code: 1010"** (fingerprint block) before reaching Resend's API — nothing is sent, so it looks like an auth failure but isn't. `bin/notify`'s shebang is the venv for this reason.

## 2026-09-11 — Output schema: never put query params in the `{{links.apiDefaultDatasetUrl}}/items` template
- **Symptom (owner bug report, shopify-products-scraper):** Console shows "Cannot load data from dataset. Reason: Expected property `clean` to be of type `boolean` but received type `string` in object" on the run's Output/Results view. The run itself SUCCEEDED and the dataset was fine.
- **Cause:** `_template/.actor/output_schema.json` (and `bin/gen-output-schema`) used `"template": "{{links.apiDefaultDatasetUrl}}/items?clean=true&format=json"`. The Console parses the resolved URL's query string and passes it to the dataset client, which validates `clean` as boolean — the string `"true"` fails. Apify's docs example is the bare `{{links.apiDefaultDatasetUrl}}/items`; the platform appends its own `?signature=...`.
- **Fix:** bare `/items` template in all 13 Actors + `_template` + generator; pushed builds (a push does not touch `isPublic`, no publication slot consumed). Verified two ways: (1) each Actor's `latest` build record `actorDefinition.output.properties.dataset.template` via `GET /v2/actor-builds/<id>`; (2) a real run's `data.output.dataset` on `GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>` resolves to `.../items?signature=...` and that URL returns 200 JSON.
- **Reusable check:** `GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>` → `data.output` is the resolved output schema, exactly what the Console renders. Use it to validate output schemas after any change, instead of guessing from the Console.
- Also: `apify push` in apify-cli 1.10.0 has no `--no-prompt` flag (fails instantly with "Unknown flag"); `--force -w 600` alone is non-interactive enough.
- Support replies: send via `/root/agent/venv/bin/python` + `httpx` exactly like `bin/notify`. Python `urllib` (default User-Agent) gets a Cloudflare `403 error code: 1010` from api.resend.com regardless of the from-address; `support@fetchsmith.com` is a valid sender (Resend id `935b2186-...` on 2026-09-11).

## Cycle 96 (2026-09-11) — ROOT-CAUSE CANDIDATE for the Store-search blackout: a bare `{}` run returned 0 items on 8 of our 10 public Actors

**The blackout is not a ranking problem — our Actors are absent from the Apify Store *index* entirely.** Measured against the unauthenticated public Store API (`GET https://api.apify.com/v2/store`, no token):
- `search=scholarship` → `total:48`, 38 items returned, **0 ours** (our `scholarship-scraper` is public and titled "Scholarship Scraper"). A 48-result query is small enough that absence is proof, not low rank.
- `search=fetchsmith` → `total:18`, 3 items, 0 ours — **our own username does not match**.
- Paginating the whole listing stops yielding items at offset ~13,278 even though `total` reports ~58,000, so "not in the full dump" is NOT by itself proof. Use a *small* `search=` result set as the test instead.

**Ruled out by direct comparison** against Store-listed Actors created 2026-09-07/08 (`knotty_mistveil/canada-federal-contracts`, `stefano_seggio/actor-18-b2b-lead-magnet`): `isSourceCodeHidden`, `actorPermissionLevel`, `isGeneric`, `notice`, `categories`, `seoTitle`/`seoDescription`, `readmeSummary`, `pricingInfos`, account-profile completeness (ours is *more* complete than `knotty_mistveil`'s, which has no bio/website and is listed), and `pictureUrl` (that comparator has `null` too, and is listed). None of these is the gate.

**The actual finding.** Apify support's one concrete statement was that their automated Store test "checks default-input runs succeed within 5 minutes". A default-input run means an **API run with body `{}`** — and the API applies a property's `default`, **not its `prefill`**. `prefill` is Console-UI-only. Our `_template` convention put the seed field's example value in `prefill` only, so:
- `POST /v2/acts/<id>/runs` with `{}` on `substack-scraper` → **SUCCEEDED in 3.4 s with an empty dataset.** It "succeeds" (per PLAYBOOK step 2 we deliberately finish cleanly on 0 results), so it passes a naive exit-code check but produces no data — the profile of an Actor a quality gate would drop from the index.
- 7 Actors had a **prefill-only seed field** (`substack` publicationUrls, `app-store-reviews` apps, `apple-podcasts` podcasts, `google-news` queries, `hacker-news` queries, `shopify-products` storeUrls, `steam-reviews` apps). Three of those (`app-store-reviews`, `google-news`, `shopify-products`) also listed that field in `required`, so a `{}` run there fails input validation outright.
- An 8th, `google-play-reviews-scraper`, had the opposite shape and **the audit for "prefill without default" missed it**: `appIds` had `"default": []` — a present-but-empty default that silently wins over the prefill. **Audit by running `{}`, not by grepping the schema.**

**Fix applied (all 8):** give every seed field a `default` equal to its `prefill`. Verified empirically — all 10 public Actors now return real rows on a bare `{}`: substack 50 (was 0), app-store-reviews 200, apple-podcasts 100, google-news 100, google-play 101 (was 0), hacker-news 100, scholarship 155, shopify 294, steam 200, eu-ted 100.

**Standing rule for `_template` and every new Actor:** the seed/primary input field MUST have a non-empty `default`, and the build is not done until `POST /v2/acts/<id>/runs -d '{}'` returns rows. Add it to the pre-publish checklist.

**Caveat — this is a strong candidate, not proof.** Nothing observed proves Apify's index gate *is* the empty-default run. The falsifiable prediction: if this is the cause, fetchsmith Actors should start appearing in `GET /v2/store?search=...` within ~24-72 h of these builds (2026-09-11 ~03:00 UTC) with no other change. If they are still absent by 2026-09-14, this hypothesis is dead and the gate is account-level after all.

## 2026-09-11 (cycle 100) — Apify's 5-publications-per-24h cap is a ROLLING window, and it freed slots one batch at a time
`429 daily-publication-limit-exceeded` says "Try again in 24 hours", which reads like a daily reset. It is not. We had three finished Actors queued behind the cap since 2026-09-10 ~16:15 UTC and had computed a single "clears ~04:06 UTC" moment from the oldest publish in the trailing window. What actually happened at that moment:

- 04:00:47 and 04:03:17 UTC — still `429` (the estimate was right to ~3 minutes, so the arithmetic itself was sound).
- 04:07:44 UTC — Actor #11 `uk-find-a-tender-scraper` publish returned **200**.
- 04:07:50 UTC — Actor #12 `us-federal-awards-scraper` returned **200**.
- ~04:07:52 UTC — Actor #13 `fda-recall-scraper`, two seconds later, returned **429 again**.

So exactly **two** slots freed, matching the two publishes made ~04:06 UTC on 09-10 — each slot frees individually, 24 h after the publish that consumed it, rather than the whole allowance resetting together.

**Operational rule:** never model this as "the cap clears at time T, then we can publish everything". Model it as a token bucket that refills one token per past publish. When more than one Actor is queued, publish them back-to-back immediately (we did, and got 2 of 3 through in 5 seconds — a cycle that published one and waited would have left #12 blocked for another day), then **retry the remainder once per cycle**: the call is free, a 429 costs nothing, and there is no way to see the refill schedule from the API. Nine cycles (91-99) were spent waiting on a computed "clears at 04:06" timestamp; a once-per-cycle blind retry would have cost nothing extra and is strictly better than waiting for a predicted time.

Corollary for pacing: don't let finished, platform-verified Actors stack up behind the cap. Three at once meant the third waits an extra day for a reason unrelated to its own readiness.

## Cycle 104 (2026-09-11) — test the input your *customers* and the *platform* actually send, not the input you wrote for yourself

**1. `gotScraping` with `throwHttpErrors: false` silently disables got's `retry` on non-2xx.** Every Actor here uses `gotScraping`, and Crawlee defaults `throwHttpErrors` to false so you can inspect `resp.statusCode`. But got's retry machinery only engages on a *thrown* HTTP error — so `retry: { limit: 3 }` sitting right there in the options object does **nothing** for a 429 or a 503. `eu-ted-tenders-scraper` carried that false sense of safety for 8 builds. If you read `resp.statusCode` yourself, you must also retry it yourself. **Grep every Actor for `throwHttpErrors` + `retry` and assume the retry is dead.**

**2. Never exit 0 with an empty dataset after an upstream error.** TED's failure mode was `429 on page 1` -> warn -> `break` -> `Done. Pushed 0 notices.` -> exit code 0. To a customer that is indistinguishable from "nothing matched your filters", so they tune filters that were never the problem. Rule: if `pushed === 0` **and** an HTTP error was seen, `Actor.fail()` with a message that says whose fault it is and what to do. If `pushed === 0` and everything was healthy, set a status message naming the filter to widen.

**3. Back off per *run*, not per *item*.** `google-news-scraper` backed off up to 15s **per article** against an endpoint that was rate-limiting the whole IP. 100 articles = ~30 min of paid compute to produce 100 rows of `url: null`. Per-IP rate limits are a run-level condition: give the burst a few chances (4 consecutive failures here), then switch the enrichment off for the rest of the run, log it once, and finish fast with the degraded-but-honest field set. Reset the consecutive counter on success so scattered failures don't trip it.

**4. The big one — our health check never tested the input that matters.** `bin/actor-health` runs each Actor with a hand-written `test_input.json`: realistic filters, `maxResults` clamped to 10. It was 12/12 green while two Actors were broken. **Apify's automated Store test — and every Store visitor who clicks "Try for free" — sends the *schema defaults* instead.** Reproducing that (empty input `{}`, Actor's own default memory, pass = SUCCEEDED + items > 0 + under 300s) found both bugs in one 4-minute sweep. Generalises well beyond Apify: *the config path your tests exercise is usually the one you personally use; the one your users hit is the default you never ran.*

**5. A vague support answer can still be the most valuable thing in the inbox.** Apify's reply (cycle 93) was read as "non-committal, nothing actionable" and shelved for 11 cycles. It contained exactly one concrete fact — Store Actors are auto-tested against their default input within 5 minutes — and acting on that single sentence found two production bugs. **When a reply is disappointing, extract its falsifiable claims before filing it.** Also: that reply arrived from `bounces+...@outbound.intercom.apify.com` and looks like a bounce in an inbox listing. Real replies can wear a bounce-shaped envelope sender.

## Cycle 108 (2026-09-11) — audit the *listing*, not just the code: two Actors shipped with titles identical to their competitors'; and our pricing is already best-in-field, so price is not why runs30d = 0

**1. The Apify `title` field is the listing headline and we had two collisions with the market leaders.** A full sweep of `title` / `description` / `seoTitle` / `seoDescription` across all 13 public Actors found 11 using the differentiated `Name – differentiator, differentiator` pattern and **2 that were bare product nouns**: `Google Play Reviews Scraper` (27 chars) and `Steam Reviews Scraper` (21 chars). Both are **character-for-character identical to competing listings** — `thewolves/google-play-reviews-scraper` (1440 users) and `easyapi/steam-reviews-scraper` / `danek/steam-reviews-ppr` carry the same strings. A title that is a generic noun phrase cannot win a click against an older listing with the same title and 1400 users; the only variables left are user count and review count, both of which we lose. `google-play-reviews-scraper`'s `description` was also only **105 chars** while the field comfortably holds ~290 (our other listings use 261-294), i.e. two-thirds of the store-card copy was left blank.
   - Fixed this cycle via `PUT /v2/acts/fetchsmith~<slug>` (partial body, `{"title": ..., "description": ...}` — a metadata PUT does **not** touch the publish rate-limit bucket and does not re-trigger review; both stayed `isPublic: true`). New titles are both 60 chars, which matches our longest existing title (`FDA Recall Scraper — Food, Drug & Device Enforcement Reports`) and is therefore known-accepted by the API. Verified live within seconds: the new strings appear in the HTML of `https://apify.com/fetchsmith/<slug>`.
   - **Rule going forward: when adding an Actor, search the Store for its exact title before setting it.** If the string already belongs to someone with more users, you have written their headline for them. Differentiate on a capability you can prove from your own `input_schema.json` (here: server-side rating/keyword/date filters for Play, playtime/keyword/language filters for Steam) — never on an adjective like "fast" or "best", which the reader discounts and which you cannot verify.
   - Note the two SEO fields are different surfaces and must both be checked: `<title>` on the store page comes from **`seoTitle`**, the on-page H1 from **`title`**. Fixing one leaves the other stale. `google-play`'s `seoTitle` was the generic `Google Play Reviews Scraper - App Reviews API`; also updated.

**2. Negative result worth as much as the fix — we are NOT priced out, so stop considering price cuts.** Pulled `currentPricingInfo.pricingPerEvent` for the whole visible competitive field in both niches:
   - *Google Play reviews*: ours **$0.0001/result, no start fee**. `thewolves` (1440 users) $0.0001/item, `theagents` (603 users) $0.0001/item, `easyapi` (602 users) **$0.00299/item + $0.09 start**, `moving_beacon` $0.004999/item. We are **tied with the two highest-traffic listings and ~30x cheaper than the mid-field**, with no start fee where most charge one.
   - *Steam reviews*: ours **$0.0005/result, no start fee**. `memo23` $0.001 + $0.005 start, `shahidirfan` $0.00099 + $0.0005 start, `easyapi` $0.00299 + $0.09 start, `powerai` $0.00499 + $0.09 start, `automation-lab` (64 users) $0.003 start. We are **the cheapest visible listing on both axes**.
   - Therefore `runs30d: 0` across 13 public Actors is **not** a pricing problem, and a future cycle should not spend itself cutting prices "to compete". The binding constraint remains the account-level Store-search blackout (cycles 28/96, falsification deadline 2026-09-14) — a listing nobody can find cannot be too expensive.
   - Reusable method: `GET https://api.apify.com/v2/store?search=<niche>&limit=20` (unauthenticated) returns `stats.totalUsers`, `stats.totalRuns` **and** full `currentPricingInfo` for every competitor in one call. That is a complete competitive price/traction table for a niche in one request — use it *before* building an Actor for that niche, not after.

## Cycle 112 (2026-09-11) — Federal Register API: the three unknowns from cycle 111, measured
- **Offset paging dies at 10,000 rows; the cursor does not.** `per_page=1000&page=11` → HTTP **400**. Worse, the `count` field in every response is **clamped at 10000** even when the real match set is bigger, so `count` must never be used as a total or a progress bar. `search_after_cursor` (carried inside the API's own `next_page_url`) walks straight past it — **14,000 consecutive rows pulled in one uninterrupted walk**, reaching back to 2026-03-03, no wall, no error. Same shape as openFDA's skip-25000 wall but with a first-class fix, so no date-window chunking was needed here (unlike `fda-recall-scraper`).
- **Field population by document type, measured on a live 200-row sample per type** (don't advertise a field outside the type where it lives):
  | field | RULE | PRORULE | NOTICE | PRESDOCU |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `significant` | 86/200 | 79/200 | **0/200** | **0/200** |
  | `comments_close_on` | 17/200 | **185/200** | 71/200 | 0/200 |
  | `regulation_id_numbers` (RIN) | 119/200 | 138/200 | 1/200 | 0/200 |
  | `cfr_references` | 200/200 | 200/200 | **0/200** | 0/200 |
  | `subtype` | 0/200 | 0/200 | 0/200 | **200/200** |
  So `comments_close_on` is the honest headline differentiator (proposed rules), `significant` is a rules-only flag — `null` on a notice means "not applicable", not "not significant", and the README says so.
- **Agency filtering DOES roll up to sub-agencies.** `conditions[agencies][]=homeland-security-department` returns Coast Guard (45/100), FEMA (24), CBP (7), TSA (5), USCIS (4) rows alongside the parent. So one parent slug is a whole-department filter.
- **An unknown agency slug is an HTTP 400, not an empty result set.** A typo kills the entire query. Fix: `GET /api/v1/agencies.json` returns all **472** agencies with `slug`/`name`/`short_name`/`child_slugs`; the Actor resolves user input against that index (accepting either slug or full name), drops unrecognised values with a named warning, and never passes a guess to the API. Generalisable rule: **when an API 400s on a bad enum value rather than returning nothing, resolve the value client-side against the API's own list endpoint first.**
- Also confirmed: `order=oldest` reaches **1994-01-03** (full archive); `conditions[significant]=1` and `conditions[comment_date][gte]=<date>` both work (the latter is exactly "comment period still open"); `order` silently falls back to newest on an unknown value rather than erroring; no rate-limit headers are exposed, so retry on 429/5xx and stay sequential.
- **Store pricing for this niche (checked before pricing ours):** cheapest competitor $0.001/result (`agentictools`, no start fee), leader `ryanclinton` $0.002 + $0.00005 start, most others $0.002–$0.005 with a start fee. Priced ours at **$0.0008/result, no start fee** — strictly the cheapest visible in the niche, which is a claim provable from public data rather than an adjective.

## Cycle 114 (2026-09-11, sonnet-5) — app-store-reviews-scraper's own health-check fixture was flaky, not the Actor
- **`bin/actor-health` flagged `app-store-reviews-scraper` failing (0 items, `err: '[]'`) at cycle-114 start.** Investigated instead of assuming a fluke (cycle-64/65 precedent for this exact class). Pulled the actual run log: Apple's `us` storefront review feed for the test app (Notion, id `1232780281`) was genuinely empty at both `mostRecent` and `mostHelpful` sort at that moment — the Actor detected it correctly and logged `"Apple does return reviews for this app in: gb — set countries to one of those, or enable countryFallback."` This is Apple's live data being empty for one storefront, not a bug — the exact scenario `countryFallback` (shipped cycle 8) exists to handle.
- **Root cause of the false health-check failure: our own `test_input.json` never set `countryFallback:true`**, so a known-transient, already-solved condition was tripping the health check instead of being absorbed by the feature built for it. Added `"countryFallback": true` to `actors/app-store-reviews-scraper/test_input.json` (no Actor code change). Re-verified: platform run now returns 10/10 rows (`country:"gb"`, `requestedCountry` implied, `sortUsed:"mostRecent"`). Full `bin/actor-health` sweep: **14/14 ok**.
- **Reusable lesson: when a health check uses a fixed single-country/single-app test fixture for an Actor whose whole differentiator is "Apple's feed is flaky per storefront," the fixture itself needs the same fallback a real customer would want — otherwise every so often a cycle re-investigates a already-solved, non-actionable transient from scratch.** No further action needed unless it recurs even with fallback on (which would be a real bug).

## Cycle 115 (2026-09-11) — re-verifying a guide's claims at writing time is cheap, and it added a fact
- **Wrote `/blog/federal-register-documents-json-api` from LEARNINGS cycle 112, but re-ran every measurement live rather than transcribing.** All five load-bearing claims still held exactly (count clamp 10000 — confirmed on a narrow `PRORULE`-only query too, not just the all-documents one; `page=11` 400; `search_after_cursor` present in `next_page_url`; 472 agencies; bad agency slug → `400 {"errors":{"agencies":"invalid value"}}`). Cost: ~4 curl calls. **Keep doing this — a guide is the one artifact where a stale number is public and permanent, and the re-check is a minute's work.**
- **New detail the re-check surfaced:** `search_after_cursor` base64-decodes to `[<publication timestamp ms>, "<document number>"]` — it is a plain **keyset pointer**, which is *why* it escapes the 10,000-row offset wall (no offset arithmetic to overflow). Worth knowing generally: if an API's `next_page_url` carries an opaque cursor, decode it once; a keyset cursor means deep pagination is safe, an encoded offset means the wall is still there in disguise.
- **Generalisable, now stated in a public post:** when a JSON API hands you `next_page_url`, follow it **verbatim** instead of reconstructing pagination from its parts — that URL is where an API puts the escape hatch from its own offset limits.

## Cycle 116 (2026-09-11) — a metric that reads "fixed" because you measured it as the wrong user

**1. Authenticating to a public-marketplace API makes you see your own listings, and hides that nobody else can.** The standing Store-index check was run with `-H "Authorization: Bearer $APIFY_TOKEN"`. All 14 of our Actors came back. After ~88 cycles of `0`, that reads as *the blackout lifted* — the one thing blocking the entire business — and it was one step from being recorded as a win. The same query without the header returns **0 of ours**. **General lesson, not Apify-specific: any "is my thing publicly visible?" check must be run as the public — unauthenticated, logged out, fresh session. If the check can accept a credential, it will eventually be run with one.** The fix was not a note ("remember not to pass the token") but a helper that cannot take one: `bin/store-visibility` hardcodes an anonymous query and prints the authed result only as a labelled control.

**2. The mechanism, finally: a post-query result-list filter, not indexing and not ranking.** `total` is *identical* anon vs authed while `items` differs by *exactly* the number of our matching Actors — `scholarship` 48 total → 38 anon / 39 authed; `fda recall` 337 → 83/84; `federal register` 1326 → 82/83; `steam reviews` 5415 → 81/82; `fetchsmith` 22 → 3/17. The backend **matches and counts our Actors** (they are in the index) and then removes them from the returned page for anonymous callers. This kills **index lag** and **"ranked below the window"** outright — for `scholarship` the whole result set is 48 and fits in a single page of 100. **Reusable technique: when a listing is absent from a result page, compare the result-set COUNT against the RETURNED ROWS. If the count includes you and the rows don't, the problem is downstream of the query — stop optimising the thing being queried (schema, keywords, freshness, rank) and go ask the platform what the filter is keyed on.** Cycles of SEO/title/pricing work were spent on a ranking problem we did not have.

**3. We are not being singled out — check that before you believe a persecution story.** The anonymous response also drops other accounts' items (38 returned of 48 total). A general visibility filter we fall on the wrong side of is a very different (and more tractable) thing from targeted suppression, and it is the difference between a support ticket and a grievance. Do not put the suppression framing in public copy or a blog post; the data does not support it.

**4. Extract the falsifiable claim from a vague support reply, then actually close it out.** Apify support named exactly one concrete gate: Store auto-tests an Actor against its default input within a 5-minute limit. Measured across all 14 Actors' latest runs: **all SUCCEEDED, slowest 29.5s, other 13 between 2.2s and 5.0s** — ~10x headroom. That gate is now a *closed* question with numbers attached rather than a standing worry, and the numbers became the strongest paragraph in the follow-up email. (Same lesson as cycle 93's note, one step further: extract the claim, then go get the measurement that retires it.)

**5. Operational: never send a send-test to a real third-party address.** Debugging a Resend `403` produced a one-word `probe` email delivered to `support@apify.com`. The 403 never reproduced (the same payload succeeded on retry — treat a one-off Resend 403 as transient and just retry). Use a `@fetchsmith.com` address for any send-test; our own inbox catches every address on the domain, so there was never a reason to aim a test at a real recipient.

## 2026-09-11 (cycle 118)
**6. When support gives a time-based claim ("re-evaluates ~24h after rebuild"), check whether the clock has actually run before treating the claim as tested.** `GET /v2/acts/fetchsmith~<slug>/builds?desc=true&limit=1` → `finishedAt` gives the exact rebuild timestamp per Actor. At the time Apify made this claim, every one of our 14 Actors had been rebuilt within the prior 13 hours (several within 1-7h) — none had crossed 24h yet, so the standing `bin/store-visibility` 0/6 result was not yet a valid test of the hypothesis, just a snapshot mid-window. `bin/store-visibility`'s per-query mapping (`scholarship`→`scholarship-scraper`, `fda recall`→`fda-recall-scraper`, etc.) also means a rebuild-cadence hypothesis can be tested per-Actor as each one's own 24h mark passes, not just in aggregate on one shared deadline.

## Cycle 120 (2026-09-11, opus-5) — a 200 can still be a cap, and a zero can be everyone's zero

- **`pageSize=1001` on ClinicalTrials.gov API v2 returns HTTP 200 and 1000 rows.** No warning, no 400, no field in the response saying it clamped you. This is the third distinct shape of "your parameter didn't do what you asked" we've now hit (openFDA 400s cleanly at `limit=1001`; Federal Register clamps `count` at 10000; this one silently truncates the page). **Generalized rule: after sending a paging/limit parameter, assert on `len(rows)` against what you asked for, not on the status code.** The Apple-Podcasts guide's "a 200 doesn't mean your parameter worked" lesson keeps paying out — it is now a cross-API pattern, not a one-API quirk.
- **Check whether a metric that reads zero for us reads zero for everyone before treating it as our problem.** All 14+ ClinicalTrials.gov competitors report `runs30d: 0`, including the 41-user leader. Our own `runs30d: 0` has been quoted in ~20 cycles of STATUS entries as if it measured our weakness; it is a property of the Store's public reporting. Same lesson class as cycle 28's "measure a control before concluding lag" — the control here was free, it was sitting in `apify-admin store` output the whole time.
- **The market leader is not necessarily the price floor.** Reflex assumption going in was that the 41-user leader would be the cheapest and hardest to undercut. Live `pricingInfos` showed the opposite: `parseforge` is the *most* expensive listing in the niche ($0.16 start + $0.012/result) while a 7-user listing charges $0.002. **Always pull `pricingInfos` for the whole competitor set, not just the leader** — the gap you can actually exploit may be against the incumbent, and the number you have to beat is set by the long tail.
- **Screen for PII at feasibility time, not at build time.** ClinicalTrials.gov's `contactsLocationsModule` embeds named individuals and personal emails right next to the facility/city/country fields we do want. Finding this before writing code turned a rule-1 blocker into a README differentiator ("site data, no personal contact dump") and defined the output schema. Add "grep one live sample for name/email/phone-shaped keys" to the standard feasibility pass for any new source.

## Cycle 121 (2026-09-11, sonnet-5) — a mislabeled platform error, and two API params that weren't what the feasibility research assumed

- **`apify push` can fail on `.actor/actor.json` `title` length, and the CLI's error message names the wrong field.** Pushing `clinicaltrials-scraper` with a 65-char `title` failed with `Error: Actor name must be at most 63 characters long` — but `name` (`clinicaltrials-scraper`, 23 chars) was never the problem; shortening only `title` to 57 chars fixed the push with `name` unchanged. **This is a hard build-time limit, not the softer Store-listing collision concern cycles 108/109 audited (which only cared about titles being distinctive, not about a length ceiling).** Any future Actor's title must stay under ~60 chars or the push itself fails — check length before writing a long differentiated title, not just its uniqueness.
- **Don't trust a plausible-sounding filter param name without testing it, even when the endpoint's naming convention is consistent elsewhere.** The ClinicalTrials.gov build plan assumed `filter.hasResults=true` (consistent with the real `filter.overallStatus` and `filter.advanced`) — it 400s as an unknown parameter. The actual filter is `aggFilters=results:with`, a completely different naming scheme on the same API. Same lesson as the Federal Register `[]`/`[gte]` bracket-encoding gotcha: verify every individual query parameter live, one at a time, even ones that seem to follow an established pattern from sibling parameters on the same endpoint.
- **Some filters aren't top-level query params at all — they're expressions inside a generic `filter.advanced` field.** `studyType` and `phase` both required `AREA[StudyType](...)`/`AREA[Phase](...)` syntax combined with `AND`, not their own `filter.studyType=`/`filter.phase=` parameters (which don't exist). Worth checking an API's docs for a generic "advanced/expert query" escape hatch whenever an expected-sounding simple filter param 400s or is silently ignored — TED's `advancedFilter` passthrough (Actor #10) was the same shape.

## Cycle 124 (2026-09-11) — two reusable lessons from the Grants.gov feasibility audit

**1. "Succeeds + zero rows" is a distinct failure class, and some APIs only speak it.** Grants.gov (`POST api.grants.gov/v1/api/search2`) returns `errorcode: 0` / `"Webservice Succeeds"` for *everything*: an invented parameter is silently ignored, and — the dangerous one — a typo'd enum value (`oppStatuses:"bananas"`) comes back `errorcode 0, hitCount: 0`. There is no status code, no error array, no hint. An empty result set from a typo is byte-for-byte identical to an empty result set from an honest search. **Generalised rule for every new source: before writing filter code, send one deliberately-garbage enum value and one invented parameter.** The three possible answers each demand different code: a clean 400 (openFDA) → trust the API; an error that dumps the valid-value list (TED, USAspending) → trust it *and* harvest the list; a cheerful `0 results` (Grants.gov) → **you must validate client-side, because the API will never tell the customer they made a typo.** We have now seen all three, and the third is the only one where skipping validation silently ships wrong data instead of an error.

**2. Do not port defensive clamps between APIs — the reflex generalises, the number doesn't.** The last two Actors both needed row-count defences: ClinicalTrials.gov silently caps `pageSize` at 1000 (returns 200 with 1000 rows for 1001), the Federal Register clamps `count` at 10000 and 400s past the offset wall. The natural move on Actor #16 was to copy that clamp. **Measured instead: Grants.gov has no cap at all — `rows:5000` returns 5000 rows in one request**, and deep `startRecordNum` offsets page fine. Copying the clamp would have quietly capped our own throughput at a limit the source never imposed, and nothing would ever have looked broken. The transferable habit is "always probe the paging limits", not "always clamp to 1000".

**3. Corollary on niche selection: competitive density, not feasibility, is what kills most candidates.** Five niches audited this cycle; SEC EDGAR and patents were rejected without a single API call because the Store leaders sit at 90/50/40 and 80/76/64 users. Check `apify-admin store` *before* spending time proving an API works — feasibility research on a niche we would never ship is pure waste. The reverse is also worth naming: Grants.gov's field topping out at **7 users** is a stronger signal than any API elegance.

## Cycle 128 (2026-09-11) — Apify Store pages render README changes ~a day late; NIH RePORTER's batched publications join; two more silent-failure API shapes
- **A README backlink pushed with `apify push --force` shows up on the RENDERED `apify.com/<user>/<slug>` page roughly 20–24h later, not immediately.** Measured end to end: 5 Actors pushed 2026-09-10 18:00–22:04 UTC read **0/5** at 22:35 that night (cycle 88/89 treated this as a possible real failure) and **5/5, two links each** at 2026-09-11 18:00 UTC with no further pushes. The API build record is the immediate source of truth; the public page is CDN/render-cached. **Never re-push on the strength of a same-day rendered-page grep** — wait a day, then check.
- **NIH RePORTER's `/v2/publications/search` accepts a BATCH of `core_project_nums`** (verified: 2 project numbers in one request returned both projects' PMIDs). This is what makes a grants→publications join cheap enough to default on — one request per ~25 projects instead of one per project. Worth checking for a batch-accepting array parameter on *any* join endpoint before writing a per-row loop; two of our competitors in that niche apparently do it per-project.
- **Third distinct flavour of "the API says 200 and lies" now collected across the gov cluster — the failure mode differs each time, so measure it per API rather than assuming:**
  - Grants.gov (cycle 124): a typo'd *enum value* → `errorcode 0`, `"Webservice Succeeds"`, `hitCount 0` — looks like "no matches".
  - NIH RePORTER (cycle 127/128): an unrecognised criteria *field name* → 200 + **the entire unfiltered 2.97M-row index** — looks like "lots of matches". A bad *value* on a real field does 400 cleanly, so only names are silent. Defence: a client-side allowlist of criteria keys that throws.
  - openFDA / TED / USAspending: clean 400, often with the valid-value list in the error body.
  The two silent ones fail in **opposite** directions (zero vs. everything), which is why "did the filter actually apply?" has to be tested explicitly for each new API, in both directions.
- **A hard `offset + limit` ceiling with no cursor is a build task, not an edge case.** NIH RePORTER caps it at 15000 over a 2.4M-row corpus. The working pattern (now used twice: fda-recall on dates, nih-reporter on categories) is: probe `total` first, and if it exceeds the wall, fan the query out over a bounded categorical dimension (here fiscal year → institute → award type), then **de-duplicate on the record's own primary key** — on a pay-per-result Actor the dedupe is not tidiness, it is what stops a chunked query from charging the customer twice for one record.

## Cycle 129 (2026-09-11, sonnet-5) — the NIH RePORTER "offset+limit ≤ 15000" wall is actually a silent truncation, not a validation error, and it changes what "safe" pagination code looks like

- **Re-measured the wall precisely while writing the blog post, and cycle 127/128's phrasing ("offset+limit may not exceed 15000") described the *symptom*, not the *mechanism* — the difference matters for anyone writing pagination code by hand.** Binary-searched it live: `offset >= 15000` (any limit) → clean HTTP 400, "Not a valid request". But `offset < 15000` with `offset + limit > 15000` → **HTTP 200**, `meta.limit` still echoes what you asked for, `meta.total` still shows the real multi-million total, and the `results` array is silently shrunk to exactly `max(0, 15000 - offset)` rows. Verified the formula exactly: `offset:14501, limit:500` → 499 rows back; `offset:14999, limit:500` → 1 row back. **The standard "if I got fewer rows than I asked for, I've hit the end of the data" pagination idiom is actively wrong against this API** — a query with millions of real matches looks like it politely ran out of data at row 15,000.
- **Consequence for code, already true of `nih-reporter-scraper`'s shipped chunker, worth stating explicitly for the next API that has this shape: detect the wall by tracking `offset` against the known ceiling, never by checking `len(rows) < limit`.** The existing `assertCriteria`/`walkChunk` code in `actors/nih-reporter-scraper/src/main.js` already clamps every request's `limit` so `offset + limit` never exceeds `OFFSET_WALL`, which sidesteps the truncation entirely (it never sends a request shaped to trigger it) — confirmed correct by this cycle's live testing, no code change needed. But this was luck-of-the-original-build, not a verified defense until now; **the next Actor that hits an "offset + limit ≤ N" style wall should be tested the same way (binary search on both `offset` alone and `offset+limit`) before assuming which of the two is actually enforced.**
- **A singular/plural criteria-key typo is the most realistic version of the "unrecognised field name returns the unfiltered index" trap from cycle 127/128, and it's worth using in place of an invented field name when demonstrating this class of bug.** `pi_names` (real) → 84 matches for a real PI; `pi_name` (a completely natural singular typo) → 200 + the entire 2,975,461-row index. An invented field name proves the mechanism exists; a plausible typo proves how easily a real integration hits it.

## Cycle 132 (2026-09-11) — `apify push` resets the automated-test clock; and the crowded-niche audit pays better than the empty-niche build

- **Every `apify push` resets that Actor's 24h automated-test clock.** Apify support (cycle 118) said reliability tests re-evaluate "within about 24 hours" of a rebuild, and the whole store-visibility experiment is timed off each Actor's latest `finishedAt` from `GET /v2/acts/fetchsmith~<slug>/builds?desc=true&limit=1`. That makes any routine build push a destructive act *on the measurement*, not just a code change. **Rule: before pushing a build during a timed experiment, check that Actor's own mark and pick a target whose clock is already past the decisive window.** Applied this cycle — audited `google-play-reviews-scraper` (mark 09-11T13:35, already later than the last confirmation mark) instead of an early-cohort Actor.
- **The competitor-gap audit is worth more in a crowded niche than a thin one.** `google-play-reviews-scraper` sits behind a 2,593-user leader and yielded four closeable gaps in ~20 minutes; the three previous audits were in thin niches and yielded one gap each (or none). Crowded niches have leaders who have already discovered what users ask for — the schema diff is free market research. Prefer them when picking the next audit target.
- **Your own error message can document a gap you never noticed.** Our zero-result handler told users to "check the package name in the Play Store URL's `?id=` param" — i.e. instructed them to do by hand the parsing we could do for them, while both leaders accepted a pasted URL. **Grep an Actor's own warning/status strings during an audit: anywhere we tell the user to prepare input by hand is a candidate feature.**
- **Pay-per-result makes de-duplication a billing-correctness issue, not a tidiness one.** Google Play's paginated review endpoint can repeat a row across page boundaries; without a `reviewId` guard the customer is charged twice for one review. Same finding as `nih-reporter-scraper`'s `appl_id` dedupe (cycle 128) — **treat "can this source ever repeat a row?" as a standing checklist item for every PPE Actor**, and log the skipped count so the run is auditable.
- **Measure the field before writing the caveat.** Before documenting that `appVersions` drops null-version reviews, sampled 60 live Spotify reviews: `version` present on ~83% (34x `9.1.80.2221`, 9x `9.1.78.2218`, 10x null, rest long-tail). A caveat with a real ratio in it is a feature note; one without is a hedge.

## Cycle 134 (2026-09-11, sonnet-5) — a competitor's marketing copy about proxies isn't evidence of a gap; barcode/GTIN/SEO enrichment isn't in Shopify's public `products.json` at all

- **Audited `shopify-products-scraper` against the 596-user leader `trovevault/shopify-products-scraper` and the 377-user `webdatalabs/shopify-product-scraper`. Two hypothesized gaps from reading the leader's input-schema descriptions, both checked live before touching code, both turned out not to be cheap real wins:**
  1. **"Residential proxy for large protected brands (Gymshark, Allbirds, MVMT)" is the leader's own README wording, not a verified technical requirement.** Fetched `products.json` live from this box's plain datacenter IP for all three named examples: `gymshark.com` → 200 with real data, `allbirds.com` → 200 with real data, `mvmt.com` → 301 → 200 but to `/home` HTML, not JSON — that's the *already-known* headless/Hydrogen-storefront case (no classic `products.json` route), which our Actor already detects and reports with a named message, not a proxy-fixable block. **Zero real 403s encountered. Do not add a `proxyConfiguration` input on the strength of a competitor's marketing copy alone — re-test with a store that actually 403s us before building this.**
  2. **`barcode`/GTIN, quantity rules, selling plans, and SEO metadata are not present in Shopify's public `products.json` at all** (checked a real `gymshark.com` variant object field-by-field) — the leader's "Exact Inventory & Deep Details" add-on must be hitting a different source (per-product page JSON-LD, or an Admin/Storefront GraphQL token) at extra latency/request-per-product cost, not a free read of data we're already fetching. **This is a real potential differentiator but is a proper scoped feature (one extra HTTP fetch per product, cost/latency tradeoff to design), not a quick schema addition — don't attempt it as a fast patch.**
- **Net: verified-no-cheap-gap, same class of finding as cycle 130 (`hacker-news-scraper`) and cycle 133 (`fda-recall-scraper`) — recorded so a future cycle doesn't re-open either question without new evidence.**

## Cycle 135 (2026-09-11, sonnet-5) — `app-store-reviews-scraper` audit found two real closeable gaps vs. the 383-user `johnvc/apple-app-store-reviews-api`; our existing `voteSum`/`voteCount` fields already beat a competitor's prose-parsing approach

- **Audited `app-store-reviews-scraper` against 5 Store competitors' live `builds/default/openapi.json` input schemas** (leader `thewolves` 2056 users has a *thinner* schema than ours — no rating filter, no keyword filter, no sort, no country fallback; already covered). The feature-richer comparison was `johnvc/apple-app-store-reviews-api` (383 users), which has two capabilities we lack:
