**Shipped the same idea, honestly labeled: `includeRiskScore` (default `true`) adds `riskScore` (0-100 int) via a plain, fully-documented weighted formula** — 45% classification severity (Class I=100/II=60/III=25/unclassified=50 neutral), 30% recency (linear decay from 100 at today to 0 at 2 years old, using `recallInitiationDate` falling back to `reportDate`), 25% distribution scope (100 for `nationwide`/`worldwide`/`international` language in `distributionPattern`, else scaled by the count of distinct US state codes found in that free-text field, 30 for 0-1 states up to 90 for 16+). README's new FAQ entry spells out the exact weights and says outright why it isn't marketed as AI: a made-up "AI" label on a documented formula would be misleading, full stop — this is a case where the honest description is also the more differentiated one (buyers can audit exactly what they're paying for, unlike the competitor's opaque "AI" claim).

**`distribution_pattern` is genuinely messy free text** — live-sampled 15 recent food-recall rows: single states/cities written out in full ("New York."), comma-separated state-code lists ("NY, NJ, PA"), narrative sentences with embedded codes ("Product was shipped to the following states: KY, NC, VA & WVA."), and explicit "Nationwide" language. The state-code-count heuristic handles all of these except a full state *name* with no code and no "nationwide" keyword (e.g. "New York." alone) — that case falls through to the 1-state-equivalent floor (30), which is actually the semantically correct outcome (it is a single-state recall), just via a coincidental path rather than name-parsing. Not worth building a 50-state name lookup table for this — the score only needs to be directionally useful, not exact.

**Verified live on build 0.1.9** (direct build-API read: `actorDefinition.input.properties.includeRiskScore` present, `actorDefinition.storages.dataset.fields.properties.riskScore` present, README FAQ text present — not the CDN-cached Store page). Real platform run, `classifications:["Class I"], reportDateFrom:"2026-01-01"` → 5 real Class I recalls, `riskScore` 80-99 on all of them (matches the formula's intent: severity alone puts a floor around 45×100=45+recency+scope, and these were all recent/multi-state). Local run with `includeRiskScore:false` confirmed the field is still present but `null` (kept in the schema for stable CSV columns, same pattern as the existing drug-only fields) — not omitted, which would have made the dataset schema lie about row shape.

**Found and fixed a real (if minor) bug in our own tooling while publishing:** `bin/apify-admin publish`'s meta.json validator capped `seoDescription` at 300 chars, but the live Apify API actually enforces **200** — the first publish attempt 400'd with `"seoDescription must be at most 200 characters long"` on a 202-char string that had passed local validation clean. A local check that under-enforces a live limit is worse than no check: it gives false confidence right up until the API call. Fixed the limit in `bin/apify-admin` to 200; every future meta.json publish now catches this locally instead of round-tripping to a live 400.

**Standing checks all clean:** `actor-health` 19/19, `check-registry-fields` 0 drift (34 fields), `check-store-meta` 0 drift after republishing meta.json, 3 services active, `/`, `/tools`, `/pricing`, `/blog`, `/tools/fda-recall-scraper` all 200. No spend beyond platform-credit test runs, no owner email, no new Actors.

## Cycle 296 (2026-09-15) — shipped `nih-reporter-scraper`'s `watchLabel`: a stateful per-saved-query "only new since last run" filter, the one real gap cycle 295 found vs `nexgendata/us-grants-funding-tracker`

**The feature, and why it is not the same as the `newlyAddedOnly` we already had.** `newlyAddedOnly` reads NIH's own *stateless* `newly_added_projects_only` flag: "recently added to the index", index-wide, which keeps re-returning the same ~8.9k projects on every run until they age out of the flag. `watchLabel` keeps the buyer's *own* baseline: the set of `appl_id`s already delivered under that label, so a scheduled run returns only what it has never sent before. That is the difference between "NIH published something recently" and "something new matched *my* query" — the second is what a weekly alert actually needs.

**Design decisions worth reusing for the next Actor that gets a watch mode:**
- **Named KV store, not the default one.** `Actor.openKeyValueStore('fetchsmith-nih-watch')` — the default KV store is per-run and would silently reset the baseline every time, which reads as "everything is new, every run" i.e. maximum charge. The named store lives on the *buyer's* account, so their saved queries never touch ours.
- **The key is `watch-<sanitised label>-<sha1(criteria)[:10]>`.** Apify KV keys allow only `[a-zA-Z0-9!-_.'()]`, so a colon separator (the obvious choice) is invalid — the label must be sanitised, never passed through. Putting the criteria fingerprint in the key is the load-bearing part: if the buyer widens a filter, that is a different question and gets a fresh baseline, instead of dumping every row the old narrower filter happened to exclude as if it were brand new.
- **First run seeds and returns zero rows, charging nothing.** Same disclosed behaviour as the competitor. The seeding walk uses NIH RePORTER's `include_fields: ["ApplId","ProjectNum"]` (verified live this cycle — it really does return only those two fields), so seeding a query costs id-only pages instead of ~60-field project records, and skips the publications join entirely.
- **Only a row the buyer was actually charged for is marked delivered.** The `watchSeen.add()` sits *after* the successful `Actor.charge()`/`pushData()`, so a project dropped by `maxResults` or a charge limit stays "new" and comes back next run rather than being silently consumed. Suppressed rows are filtered *before* `normalize()` and before the publications join, so an already-seen project costs neither a request nor a charge.
- **The seed is capped at 15,000 = the same offset wall the rest of the Actor is built around.** A baseline that hit the cap would make the first incremental run report old rows past the cap as new, so the run log says so explicitly and the README repeats it.

**Verified across three real platform runs on build 0.1.7** (not just locally): run 1 seeded 82 projects, dataset `x-apify-pagination-total: 0`, KV record written with `lastRunStatus: seeded`; run 2 on the identical input returned 0 and logged "nothing new"; then three ids were deleted from the live KV record via the API to simulate three genuinely new projects, and run 3 returned **exactly those three**, fully normalized with the PubMed join intact, leaving the baseline back at 82 and `runCount: 3`. Locally the same three-step sequence passed first, plus two extra checks the platform can't easily show: changing one filter (`orgStates:["MA"]`) produced a *different* key and a fresh seeding run, and a no-`watchLabel` run created no watch store at all and returned its normal 10 rows (regression-clean).

**Our own `bin/apify-admin publish` validator earned its keep the same day it was fixed.** Cycle 293 tightened the `seoDescription` cap to the real 200; this cycle the *`description`* check caught a 371-char string against the 300 limit before it ever reached the API — the rewritten one is 298. A local limit check is worth the five lines exactly because it fails at the point where rewriting is cheap.

**Unrelated observation, single measurement, NOT yet a conclusion (two-cycle rule):** `actor-health` came back 18/19 with `app-store-reviews-scraper` returning `[]`. It is **upstream, not ours** — Apple's own `itunes.apple.com/.../rss/customerreviews` JSON feed returned **zero entries for every combination probed**: app 1232780281 in `us`/`gb`/`de`, and a second, much larger app (284882215) in `us`. A feed-wide empty window, the same family of flakiness as cycles 9/65/66/67. The Actor behaved correctly (finished with 0 rows, did not fail, did not charge). Re-measure next cycle before touching any code or copy; if it is still empty across a different day, that is a second measurement and the README/Store caveat can be updated then.

## Cycle 297 (2026-09-15) — porting `watchLabel` to a second Actor surfaced two bugs the first port could not have

Ported cycle 296's `watchLabel` pattern from `nih-reporter-scraper` to `federal-register-scraper`. The pattern itself copied cleanly (named KV store, label+criteria-fingerprint key, free id-only baseline run, record-as-delivered-only-after-charge, skip-before-normalize). Two things did **not** copy, and both are general:

1. **Never fingerprint a criteria value that has a *relative* default.** `federal-register-scraper`'s publication-date window defaults to a rolling "last 90 days". Fingerprinting the *resolved* dates would give a scheduled daily watch a brand-new key every single day — it would seed forever and never deliver a row, while looking perfectly healthy in the log. Fix: fingerprint the user's *explicit* input (`normDate(input.x, null)`), not the resolved value. Rule: **the fingerprint must be built from what the buyer typed, not from what the code computed.** Any Actor with a relative date default (`last N days`, `today`) has this trap.

2. **A baseline walk and a result walk have different paging needs.** Two real bugs, both found by checking the seed count against the API's own `count`, not by reading the code:
   - The seed inherited `per_page` from `maxResults` (50), so it recorded 50 of 51 matching documents. A baseline that stops early silently re-reports everything past the stopping point as "new" on the first incremental run. Seeds must pin `per_page` to the maximum — they cover the whole match set, not one page of it.
   - The Federal Register API returns `next_page_url` in **two** shapes, verified live: a `search_after_cursor` link for large walks and a plain `page=N` link for small result sets. The existing pager read only the cursor and broke out of the loop otherwise. That was harmless for plain runs (`per_page` is always >= `maxResults`, so page 1 already satisfies the request) but **wrong in watch mode**, where nearly every row is skipped as already-seen and the walk must keep going to find the new ones. Fixed with an `applyNext()` helper that follows either shape.

**Verification method that caught both:** seed, then compare `seenCount` against the API's own `count` for the identical query. Then delete ids from the live KV record — including the id in the *last* row of the *second* page — and confirm exactly those come back. The last-row choice is what proved the paging fix; with the old code the run returned 2 of 3 and still looked like a success.

**Generalisation:** when porting a stateful feature, the risky part is never the state machine, it is the host Actor's paging and defaults. Budget the cycle for re-verifying those, not for re-reading the pattern.

## Cycle 298 (2026-09-15) — `watchLabel` port #3, `grants-gov-scraper`: a third variant of "does the seed need to be expensive" and a clean instance of both known traps

**Checked both standing traps before writing a line of code, per cycle 297's note.** (1) Relative-date-in-fingerprint: `postedWithinDays` (rolling "last N days") is passed to the API as a raw day count, never resolved to an absolute date anywhere in this Actor's code — unlike `federal-register-scraper`'s rolling-90-day default, there is no computed date to accidentally fingerprint, so the raw value is safe as-is. (2) Seed-walk completeness: this API's own pagination (`startRecordNum` offset, walked to `hits.length < PAGE_SIZE`) already covers the whole match set with no cursor/page-link ambiguity (verified live cycle 124: `rows:5000` and deep offsets both work with no cap), so there was no federal-register-style paging bug to find here — a genuine clean negative on both fronts, not a missed check.

**A third, new correctness question this port surfaced that neither prior port had: does a "thin" (id-only) seed correctly represent the buyer's actual filter?** `grants-gov-scraper`'s `minAwardAmount`/`maxAwardAmount` filter can only be evaluated after a per-row detail fetch (`enrich`), which the id-only seed would normally skip for cost reasons. Skipping it anyway would be wrong, not just cheap: an opportunity whose award ceiling is *not yet set* at seed time (and therefore excluded from the true match set) could get a ceiling assigned later — a real, observed field-level edit on this API — and then genuinely start matching. If the un-enriched seed had blindly added every raw hit's `id` to the baseline (including ones that don't currently pass the amount filter), that legitimately-newly-matching opportunity would be silently marked "already seen" and would never surface as new. Fixed by forcing enrichment during seeding whenever an amount filter is set (mirroring the `enrich` variable's own forcing rule for a normal run), and skipping it only when nothing in the criteria depends on enriched fields. **The general rule: a watch-mode seed must reproduce the exact predicate a normal run would apply, not a cheaper approximation of it — cost-cutting a seed is only safe for the parts of the filter that are independent of enrichment/join steps entirely.**

**Verified live on build 0.1.9/0.1.10, 3 real platform runs, deliberately with a broad query to stress the whole-set walk:** `keyword:"water"`, all 4 statuses, no amount filter — seed recorded **18,458** opportunity ids (well under the 20,000 `SEED_CAP`, and this Actor's live-verified no-offset-wall meant the walk needed no cursor trick), 0 dataset rows, 0 charged; identical rerun → 0 new; then removed the **last 3 ids from the tail of the recorded set** (the ids scanned on the final page of the walk) directly via the KV API and reran → exactly those 3 came back. Removing from the tail rather than the middle is the check that actually proves the walk reached the end of a large (18k-row) result set, not just that dedup works on an easy case.

**Fleet status: `watchLabel` is now on 3 of 19 Actors** (`nih-reporter-scraper` cycle 296, `federal-register-scraper` cycle 297, `grants-gov-scraper` cycle 298) — enough for a cross-cutting blog post per cycle 297's queued idea. Remaining alert-shaped candidates with stable per-row ids: `fda-recall-scraper` (`recallNumber`), `clinicaltrials-scraper` (`nctId`).

## Cycle 299 (2026-09-15) — `watchLabel` port #4, `fda-recall-scraper`: multi-endpoint product-type fanout is the paging shape that matters here, both standing traps confirmed present (not clean negatives this time)

**Both standing traps were real for this Actor, unlike cycle 298's clean-negative result on `grants-gov-scraper`.** (1) `reportDateFrom`/`reportDateTo` default to a **rolling** window (`today - 365 days` .. `today`, both computed from `new Date()` at run start) — fingerprinting the resolved values would have handed a scheduled daily watch a fresh baseline every single day, seeding forever. Fixed the same way as `federal-register-scraper` (cycle 297): the criteria fingerprint uses the buyer's raw `input.reportDateFrom`/`input.reportDateTo` (or `null` if left as the rolling default), never the `normDate()`-resolved value. (2) Seed-walk completeness: this Actor already re-chunks wide date ranges into sub-windows to stay under openFDA's 25,000-row `skip` cap (`planWindows()`), and the real run's per-reader page size is derived from `maxResults` (`pageSize = min(1000, max(20, min(maxResults,200)))`) — exactly the shape of cycle 297's bug (a seed inheriting a small page size tied to `maxResults` and stopping short). Avoided by giving the seed walk its own loop at the API's max page size (1000) regardless of `maxResults`, reusing the identical `planWindows`/`fetchPage`/`buildSearch` functions the real run calls so the two walks can never page the underlying API differently.

**The enrichment-dependent-filter trap (cycle 298's new finding) was a genuine clean negative here:** this Actor has no per-row detail fetch at all — `riskScore` and every filterable field come from the same search-hit row openFDA already returns, so an id-only seed can never misrepresent a filter that depends on a later enrichment step. Confirmed by reading the whole file, not assumed.

**A new wrinkle specific to this Actor's shape: three independent endpoints (food/drug/device), each separately paginated and windowed.** The existing real-run loop already handles this with a per-product-type "reader" (buffer + window index + skip offset), round-robining rows across types so a small `maxResults` doesn't return 100% food. The seed walk doesn't need round-robin fairness (it discards everything but the id), so it was written as a flat `for (productType) { for (window) { walk skip until exhausted } }` — simpler than the reader abstraction, correct because seeding has no per-type output quota to balance. Stable id reuses the Actor's pre-existing dedup key exactly (`recall_number`, falling back to `event_id:product_description` when a row has no recall number), extracted into a shared `dedupKeyOf()` so the real loop and the seed walk can never disagree on what counts as "the same recall."

**Verified on 3 real platform runs, build 0.1.10 (code) / 0.1.11 (README), both confirmed live via direct build-API read of `actorDefinition.input.properties.watchLabel` and the README text:** seed (`classifications:["Class I"]`, `reportDateFrom:"2026-08-01"`, `reportDateTo:"2026-09-14"`, all 3 product types) → 108 recall ids recorded, 0 rows, 0 charged, `lastRunStatus: seeded`; identical rerun → 0 new, `lastRunStatus: incremental`, `runCount: 2`; removed the **last 3 ids from the tail** of the recorded set via the KV API (all 3 happened to be `device` rows, since round-robin interleaving during a normal run doesn't apply to the flat seed-recording order) → run 3 returned **exactly** those 3, `runCount: 3`, baseline back to 108. Regression check: a plain run with no `watchLabel` (`productTypes:["food"]`, `maxResults:5`) still returns 5 normal rows. Test KV record deleted afterwards.

**Fleet status: `watchLabel` is now on 4 of 19 Actors** (`nih-reporter-scraper` cycle 296, `federal-register-scraper` cycle 297, `grants-gov-scraper` cycle 298, `fda-recall-scraper` cycle 299). Also fixed a real gap while here: `grants-gov-scraper`'s registry.json summary (cycle 298) never got the "optional watchLabel mode" sentence that `federal-register-scraper`/`nih-reporter-scraper`'s summaries have — an oversight in that cycle's docs sync, left for the next cycle to fix (queued). One alert-shaped candidate remains unported: `clinicaltrials-scraper` (`nctId`).

## Cycle 300 (2026-09-15) — the cross-cutting write-up was worth more than a 5th port, and the footer standing check caught a same-day regression

**Why the post instead of the `clinicaltrials-scraper` port.** Four consecutive BUILD cycles (296–299) shipped the same feature onto four Actors. The marginal value of a 5th port is small; the marginal value of the *generalisation* is not, because standing rule cycle-16 still holds — distribution is the binding constraint, and this is the first piece of content we have that is genuinely developer-shaped (a debugging war story with four named failure modes) rather than "here is an API and its gotcha". It also links four `/tools/` pages from one page and gets four Store READMEs linking back, which no single-Actor post does.

**The honest-footer rule has teeth.** `invisible-characters-return-zero-rows.md` was published earlier the same day with a closing italic paragraph listing the Actors — which *looks* like a footer at a glance and is why the human-eye check misses it — but no AI-disclosure line. The cycle-160 grep caught it in five seconds. Two things worth keeping: (1) a closing italic block is not the same as the disclosure footer, so the check must grep for the exact phrase and not for "is there a trailing italic paragraph"; (2) the fix used the **neutral** variant, because the post's findings came from *other* cycles' runs — retro-fitting the strong "every claim above comes from a live request made while writing this post" clause would have made the disclosure itself the false statement. Never upgrade a retro-fitted footer to the strong variant.

**Cross-cutting posts should carry no `tool:` frontmatter.** `site/app.py` uses `tool:` to bind a post to exactly one `/tools/<slug>` page (both for the tool page's guide list and the post page's sidebar). A post that serves four Actors equally has no correct single value, so omit the key — the 3 pillar pages already established this convention and it should stay the rule for any multi-Actor write-up.

**Fleet status unchanged: `watchLabel` on 4 of 19 Actors.** One alert-shaped candidate remains unported (`clinicaltrials-scraper`, `nctId`), and it now has four known traps to pre-check rather than two — the enrichment-dependent-predicate trap (cycle 298) and the seed-paging trap (cycle 297) both need an explicit read of `src/main.js` before any code is written.

## 2026-09-15 (cycle 302)

**Apify's x402/Skyfire "agentic payments" is a real, current platform feature, discovered via a competitor's README, not our own research.** `foxlabs/ted-tenders` (36-user leader in the EU-tenders niche) advertises "your agent can pay for its own runs... over x402 (USDC on Base) or Skyfire — no Apify account or API token of its own." Verified via `WebSearch` against Apify's own blog/docs (`blog.apify.com/introducing-x402-agentic-payments`, `docs.apify.com/platform/integrations/x402`), not the competitor's marketing copy: Apify brought 20,000+ PAY_PER_EVENT Actors into the x402 ecosystem in partnership with Coinbase, plus a separate Skyfire integration. Eligible Actors get `allowsAgenticUsers=true` **automatically** — no manual opt-in, no code change — once ALL of: (1) pricing model is PAY_PER_EVENT only (rental/usage-based excluded; every one of our 19 Actors already qualifies), (2) the Actor doesn't request full permissions, (3) it doesn't use Standby mode, (4) **the developer account has completed Apify identity verification (KYC)** — until KYC is done, *none* of that account's Actors are eligible, regardless of the other three conditions.

**Why this is logged, not acted on: the KYC step is a hard owner-gate this worker cannot cross.** It requires a real government-issued ID tied to a real person — nothing this worker can submit or fabricate (and fabricating identity documents would be exactly the kind of thing the legal/ethical rule in `CLAUDE.md` forbids). This is the same shape as the already-deferred Polar checkout (owner action required, no current evidence of urgent buyer demand to justify interrupting them). **Do not email about this in isolation** — per the standing rule ("asking them for anything costs score"), the right move if this is ever raised is one bundled email covering both the Polar-checkout trigger and this KYC step together, only once there's a concrete reason (real buyer-intent signal, or a milestone review of the $0-revenue strategic note), not just because the feature exists. If a future cycle finds itself about to re-discover this from scratch: it's already known, parked, and the blocker hasn't changed unless `state/STATUS.md`/`tasks/queue.md` says otherwise.

**Competitor-gap re-audit method extended to a niche never vs-audited before (`eu-ted-tenders-scraper`).** Prior cycles (239/247) only ran the keyword-gap-for-SEO sweep on it, not a real feature/price comparison against a named competitor. Worth remembering when picking the next re-audit target: grep `tasks/queue_archive.md` for `"<slug>.*vs"` to distinguish "never feature-audited" from "keyword-swept but not feature-audited" — they look similar in a plain full-text search of the Actor's name but are different kinds of prior work.

**Competitor-gap re-audits should diff OUTPUT fields, not just inputs — cycle 303 found a real gap only cycles 291-293/295 hadn't looked for.** Every prior re-audit (hacker-news, clinicaltrials, fda-recall, federal-register, nih-reporter, eu-ted-tenders) compared input schemas and pricing but not output/dataset schemas. On `google-play-reviews-scraper` the input side was a clean negative (our leader-checked 3 competitors' filters are all matched or beaten, and `neatrat`'s `deviceType` filter is likely inert — the `google-play-scraper` npm library's `reviews()` call, which we and probably `thewolves`/`theagents` all wrap, has no device parameter at all in its own docs). The real, verifiable gap was in the data itself: Google Play attaches optional per-aspect sub-ratings (`criterias`, e.g. `{criteria:"vaf_app_quality_ads_frequency", rating:2}`) to a review when the reviewer answers a prompt, and the underlying library already returns it — nobody was reading it. Live-sampled twice (20 reviews from `com.spotify.music`): 6-7 of 20 rows carry it, a genuinely common field, not a rare edge case. None of the 3 real-usage competitors (`neatrat` 2622 users, `thewolves` 1462, `theagents` 611) expose it in their dataset schema (checked via the build API, not their marketing copy). Shipped as `aspectRatings` (array of `{criteria, rating}`, `[]` when absent). **Lesson for the next re-audit: pull the competitor's `actorDefinition.storages.dataset.fields.properties` via the build API alongside the input schema — a library you already depend on may be returning fields you're silently dropping in your own `map*()` function.**

## Cycle 304 (2026-09-15) — `substack-scraper` vs `automation-lab/substack-scraper` (449 users): the gap was the *pricing shape*, not fields; plus a hard platform limit on PLATINUM/DIAMOND tiers

- **Competitor-gap re-audits should diff the PRICING SHAPE (how many separately-priced events, and what triggers each), not just the numbers.** Cycles 54/182/210 all "priced-audited" `substack-scraper` against the same leader and each time compared our single post price to theirs. What none of them noticed: `automation-lab` bills **three** events — `post-metadata` ($0.00115 FREE), `post-with-content` ($0.0023 FREE) and `comment` — while we billed one flat `result` for a post whether or not the buyer asked for the article body. Our own `includeBodyText:false` path makes **no per-post detail request at all** (see `handlePost()`: `needDetail = includeBodyText || includeBodyHtml`), so a metadata-only run is genuinely cheaper for us to serve and we were charging 74% above the leader for it. Fixed by adding a `post-metadata` charge event selected once at startup (`const POST_EVENT = includeBodyText || includeBodyHtml ? 'result' : 'post-metadata'`). **Generalisable: whenever an Actor has a boolean input that skips a network request, check whether the price should fork with it.** Other candidates worth checking on a future cycle: `ats-jobs-scraper` (detail fetch per job), `grants-gov-scraper` (per-row enrichment), `apple-podcasts-scraper`.
- **HARD PLATFORM LIMIT, verified twice this cycle: on our account, PLATINUM and DIAMOND tiered PPE prices cannot be set below GOLD — the API silently normalises them UP to the GOLD value, with a 200 and no warning.** Sent `result` GOLD 0.00115 / PLATINUM 0.00078 / DIAMOND 0.00055 → live read back 0.00115/0.00115/0.00115. Re-tested with PLATINUM/DIAMOND at exactly 50% of FREE (to test a "max 50% discount" hypothesis) → clamped again, so it is not a percentage rule. It is also **not** a step-size rule: the same publish moved GOLD from 0.0015 (SILVER) down to 0.00078, a 48% step, and that stuck. Competitors' older entries (`automation-lab`, priced 2026-08-25) *do* carry distinct PLATINUM/DIAMOND values, so the platform supports the shape generally — we just can't set it. **Consequences: (1) our only lever for volume buyers is the GOLD price, which now sets the floor for GOLD/PLATINUM/DIAMOND alike; (2) any earlier cycle's "we beat them at every tier" conclusion that relied on a PLATINUM/DIAMOND number we submitted should be re-read as "we beat them down to GOLD" — go check the live GET, not `meta.json`.** Always re-read `pricingInfos[-1]` live after a publish; `meta.json` is a request, not the state.
- **New ladder, after the clamp was understood:** post-with-text 0.002/0.0018/0.0015/**0.00078 GOLD+**, post-metadata 0.00112/0.00098/0.00076/**0.00039**, comment 0.00056/0.00049/0.00038/**0.00019**. That beats `automation-lab` at FREE→PLATINUM on all three events; at DIAMOND they are cheaper (0.00056 / 0.00028 / 0.00014), which we cannot match because of the clamp — but they charge a **$0.005 Actor-start fee** and we charge none, so we stay cheaper per run below ~23 posts even at DIAMOND. Real compute cost measured from the run's own `usageTotalUsd` this cycle: $0.000078/item on a 3-item metadata-only run — ~5-10x margin even at the new GOLD+ floor.
- **`chargedEventCounts` on a run lags the run's own SUCCEEDED status by a minute or two.** Read immediately after `run-sync-get-dataset-items` returned 2 real items, the run showed `{'result': 0, 'post-metadata': 0, 'comment': 0}`; re-read ~60s later it showed `{'result': 2, ...}`. Do not conclude "charging is broken" from a zero read taken right after the sync call — re-read before investigating.

## Cycle 306 (2026-09-15) — `uk-find-a-tender-scraper` vs `ciel_labs/uk-government-tenders-contracts-finder`: another pricing-SHAPE gap, this time a trial-conversion hook rather than a forked event

- **Same class of finding as cycle 304 (`substack-scraper`), different mechanic: "first N records free per run" is a shape difference a flat-price comparison misses entirely.** `ciel_labs/uk-government-tenders-contracts-finder` charges a flat $0.008/record — nearly 3x our $0.003→$0.0025 ladder — so a naive "who's cheaper" check would have called this a clean win and moved on (which is exactly what it was, on inputs/outputs/price-level). The real differentiator in their README was a trial hook: the first 25 records of every run cost nothing, so a first-time buyer can see the actual data shape before paying for any of it. This is orthogonal to price level — a much more expensive competitor can still out-convert a cheaper one if trying it costs nothing.
- **Implementation is a pure in-Actor run-scoped counter, no KV store or persistence needed** (unlike `watchLabel`, which needs cross-run state) — "every run" in their own README means every single execution, not a lifetime-per-account allowance. `pushResult()` now tracks `freeGiven` and skips `Actor.charge()` for the first `FREE_PER_RUN` (25) matching rows, charging normally after. Trivial to verify: one run with `maxResults` comfortably above 25 (confirms the cutoff arithmetic), one run with `maxResults` below 25 (confirms zero charge isn't a first-run-only fluke — a plausible bug shape would be "free only until KV/global state initializes," which a single above-threshold run wouldn't catch).
- **Lesson for the next re-audit in the sweep (`us-federal-awards-scraper`, `fec-campaign-finance-scraper`, `scholarship-scraper`, `apple-podcasts-scraper`): read the competitor's README prose, not just their `pricingInfos` JSON.** The API only reveals the event names and per-tier numbers; a "first N free" allowance, a minimum-charge floor, or any other run-scoped rule lives entirely in the Actor's own code and is only documented (if at all) in free text. `eventTieredPricingUsd` alone would never have surfaced this gap.

## Cycle 308 (2026-09-15) — reading Googlebot logs correctly, and the mobile CTA trap in a two-column layout

**1. A collapse in Googlebot hits/day is usually the good outcome, not a de-index.** Our log went 0 → 25/21/23 → 1/1/1 over six days. Read alone, the drop to 1/day looks like a penalty. It isn't. The tell is *which URLs* the remaining hits touch: during 09-10..12 the bot crawled breadth-first over `/`, `/blog`, 10+ posts, 8+ `/tools/*`, `/about`, `/contact`, `/privacy`, `/pricing`; from 09-13 on, every single hit landed on the **newest content published since the last crawl**. That's discovery finishing and freshness polling starting. **Always diff the crawled URL *set*, never just the daily count** — and corroborate with the referral side, which is the only thing that proves the pages actually rank.

**2. Our verified-visitor test is what makes a Google referral believable.** Raw `ref LIKE '%google.%'` went 1-2/day → 7/day, which overstates it badly: **11 of 16 of those "Google visitors" never loaded our CSS** — referrer-spoofing crawlers wearing browser UAs. Only the `vid IN (SELECT vid FROM asset_hits)` subset (5 of 16) is real. Any future SEO claim must quote the verified number; the raw one is inflated by roughly 3x here.

**3. The trap worth remembering: a sticky sidebar CTA silently becomes a below-the-fold footer on mobile.** `templates/post.html` and `templates/tool.html` put the content `<div>` first and `<aside class="side">` (the only CTA on the page) second, which is correct DOM order for a desktop `position:sticky` sidebar. But `@media(max-width:820px)` collapsed the grid to one column and dropped the sticky — so on phones the CTA rendered **after the entire 60-110-line article**. Nothing in any test catches this: every page still returns 200, every link still works, `curl` sees the anchor present in the HTML. It only shows up if you (a) know the media query exists and (b) reason about source order. **Fix is one property — `.side{order:-1}` inside the media query** — because CSS grid/flex items honour `order`, so no template or markup change is needed and desktop is untouched. **Generalise: any time a layout has a `position:sticky` aside plus a `max-width` rule that unsticks it, check what source order does to the CTA.**

**4. Where the leverage actually is now.** Google ranks the *deep guides*, not the homepage — all 5 verified organic landings were on `/blog/<guide>`, zero on `/`. After ~300 cycles of Actor feature work with flat demand, this is the first channel that moved. Concentrate on-page effort on the handful of guides that actually receive traffic rather than publishing another one.

## Cycle 311 — a Store-test margin can erode from Google's own rate limiting alone, no code change on our side; `timeBudgetOk()` doesn't protect the 300s Store-test window

**Context:** QUALITY cycle. `bin/store-test`'s full sweep hadn't been re-run since cycle 289 (22 cycles). Re-ran it fleet-wide and found `google-news-scraper` — which cycle 289 already fixed once (100→50 default) — back in danger: 233s then 285s against Apify's 300s Store-test cap, down from cycle 289's measured 139s/161s margin, at the *same* 50-item default.

**Root cause: this is not a regression in our code.** The Actor's own code comment already documents that Google rate-limits the URL-decode endpoint per source IP; that limit has apparently gotten slower/stricter since cycle 289 measured it, so the same 50-item default now costs more wall-clock per article. `timeBudgetOk()` (the in-Actor guard that stops processing and returns partial results) only compares against `Actor.getEnv().timeoutAt` — the *actual configured run timeout*, which for this fleet is the platform default (much larger than 300s) — so it never fires before Apify's separate, external 300s Store-test threshold is hit. There is no code path that protects specifically against the Store test's own limit; the only lever is keeping the *default* cheap enough in wall-clock terms.

**Fix: same lever as cycle 289 — lower `maxItemsPerQuery`'s schema default further (50 → 25).** Verified live: 179s and 87s (two clean runs) vs. the pre-fix 233s/285s, at 24-25 items respectively. A third run in between returned 0 items in 2s with an Apify proxy `594 ECONNREFUSED` on the very first feed request — read the run log directly and confirmed it was a one-off infra blip (nothing about our new default caused it, and an immediate retry succeeded cleanly), not a second data point against the fix.

**Reusable takeaways:**
1. **`bin/store-test`'s full sweep needs to be re-run periodically even with zero schema changes on our side** — cycle 289's fix note said "re-run whenever an Actor's input schema changes," which is necessary but not sufficient; an *upstream* rate-limit or latency change on a wrapped API can erode the same margin with no code change at all. Treat the 15-20-cycle periodic re-run as a real regression check, not just a post-change one.
2. **An in-Actor time-budget guard tuned to the real run timeout does not protect against Apify's separate, shorter, automated Store-test threshold** — those are two different numbers (ours might be 3600s+, Store test is a fixed 300s), and conflating them is an easy trap. The only real lever for a Store-test margin is keeping the *default* input cheap, not a runtime guard.
3. **When re-verifying a fix with back-to-back live runs, an unrelated transient infra failure (0 items, exotic status/error code, sub-5s duration) is not evidence against the fix** — read the run log before concluding anything; a proxy-level `ECONNREFUSED` on the very first request, that clears on immediate retry, is the known transient-upstream shape (`app-store-reviews-scraper`'s empty-feed windows are the same pattern), not a new bug.

## Cycle 312 (2026-09-15) — JSON-LD went stale the same way boilerplate prose does: a hardcoded price in `<head>` that no check compares against the price rendered in `<body>`
`site/templates/tool.html`'s `SoftwareApplication` JSON-LD carried `"offers":{"price":"0.0025"}` as a **literal**, while the visible sidebar on the same page computed the real figure (`credits_per_result * 0.0025`). For 14 of 19 tools the two agreed by luck (1 credit/result); for the other 5 — `substack-scraper` (2 credits, $0.0050) and `eu-ted-tenders-scraper`/`uk-find-a-tender-scraper`/`us-federal-awards-scraper`/`fda-recall-scraper` (3 credits, $0.0075) — the structured data **understated the price by 2-3x and contradicted the human-readable price on the same URL**. Fixed by reusing the exact same Jinja expression already present in the body.
- **The general rule (same family as cycle 272's "grep the constants" finding, new surface):** every number that appears in *both* machine-readable metadata and rendered copy must be derived from one expression, not typed twice. `check-registry-fields` and `check-store-meta` read registry/platform metadata and were 0-drift throughout — **neither looks at template `<head>` output**, so this drifted silently for the whole life of the template.
- **Cheap standing check, no new tooling:** fetch each `/tools/<slug>`, `json.loads` the `<script type="application/ld+json">` block, and assert `offers.price == '%.4f' % (credits_per_result * 0.0025)` against `actors/registry.json`. Ran it this cycle: 19/19 parse, 0 mismatches post-fix.
- Also archived this file: at 437KB it was 3x the 150KB re-trim threshold used for STATUS/queue. Cycles 1-272 moved verbatim to `notes/LEARNINGS_ARCHIVE.md` (349KB), live file now 89KB from cycle 273 on. Verified lossless by reconstructing (`tail -n +9 archive; tail -n +7 live`) and `diff`ing against a pre-split backup — identical. **Every `notes/LEARNINGS.md cycle <N>` pointer for N < 273 written in past STATUS/queue entries now resolves in the archive, so grep both files.**

## Cycle 318 (2026-09-15) — competitor-gap re-audit on `fec-campaign-finance-scraper`, shipped a real donor-contribution search mode; FEC's Schedule A endpoint has an undocumented required-filter trap
Store leader `ryanclinton/fec-campaign-finance` (16 users, the strongest of 15+ long-tail clones — same fragmented-niche pattern as every other gov-data Actor) charges $0.002/record + a tiny actor-start fee via `pricingInfos`; our flat `$0.001/result` with no start fee was already a clean pricing win, unchanged. Its input schema (read via `GET /v2/actor-builds/<latest buildId>`) exposes a `mode` preset list (`due-diligence`, `opposition-research`, `lobbying-intelligence`, `graph`, `network-intelligence`, …) that reads as LLM-enrichment output — out of reach per `CLAUDE.md` rule 2 (no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`) — but underneath all of that sits a genuinely different, non-AI capability: a `searchMode: "contributions"` path over FEC's own **Schedule A** individual-donor-contribution data (donor name, employer, occupation, amount, date, receiving committee), which our Actor's own README FAQ had explicitly and correctly disclaimed as unsupported ("Not from this Actor. It returns candidate-level aggregates only").
- **Shipped `searchMode` (candidates | contributions), build 0.1.9 + Store republish.** New inputs `donorName`, `donorEmployer`, `minAmount`; `state`/`electionYear`/`maxResults` reused across both modes. 10 new output fields (`contributorName`, `contributorEmployer`, `contributorOccupation`, `contributorCity`, `contributorState`, `contributionAmount`, `contributionDate`, `contributorAggregateYtd`, `committeeId`, `committeeName`) — 21 → 31 total registered fields, `check-registry-fields` 0 drift.
- **Real trap in the wrapped API, worth knowing before touching this endpoint again: `GET /v1/schedules/schedule_a/` 504s ("Query timed out") on a full-table scan if `two_year_transaction_period` isn't set** — a `contributor_employer=GOOGLE` query with no cycle filter times out; the identical query with `two_year_transaction_period=2024` returns in under a second (129,917-row result set, `is_count_exact: true`). This isn't documented as `required` in any schema the API exposes — it only shows up as a live 504. Fixed by always sending it in contributions mode, defaulting to the current even year when the caller leaves `electionYear` empty (candidates mode keeps it fully optional, unaffected). **If a future Actor wraps any FEC `/schedules/` endpoint, assume an unscoped query times out and always send a cycle/date-range filter by default, even in "browse everything" mode.**
- **PII line-drawing note for this niche specifically:** Schedule A donor name/employer/occupation/city/state is the FEC's own legally-mandated public disclosure (same category the whole Actor already existed for, same fields every competitor in this niche already ships) — not scraped-behind-login personal data, so it doesn't trip the `CLAUDE.md` PII-harvesting rule. Kept it conservative anyway and deliberately left out full street address (Schedule A carries `contributor_street_1/2` too) since city/state is enough for the stated use cases (donor research, employer-concentration analysis) and matches the fleet's existing precedent of trimming a field one notch more private than the API offers (`substack-scraper`'s `support_email` omission, cycle 316).
- **Re-learned, don't repeat: `.actor/actor.json`'s `title`/`description` only apply at Actor *creation* — a later `apify push` silently ignores them even though the build succeeds** (documented `notes/LEARNINGS_ARCHIVE.md` ~line 1054; this cycle burned one extra `apify push` re-discovering it live via `check-store-meta` before remembering the fix is `meta.json` + `bin/apify-admin publish`, not a rebuild). The Store-visible title only updated after the `publish` call.
- **Verified on 4 real platform runs (build 0.1.8/0.1.9), all read back from the Datasets API, not just run status:** `donorEmployer:"Google", electionYear:2024, minAmount:1000` → 5/5 rows all ≥ $1000 (`YU, KAIWEN` $3300 x2 committees, `CHIA, DANIEL` $2000 x2, `WEINBERGER, PETER` $1000); regression `candidateName:"Warren", state:"MA", office:"S"` → 2/2 rows, byte-identical shape to pre-change output; `donorName:"Musk, Elon", state:"TX"` → 4/4 rows, all `contributorState:"TX"`; default-electionYear (no year given) → resolved to 2026, real current-cycle row (`contributionDate: "2026-06-30"`). `chargedEventCounts` on run 1 confirmed `{'result': 5}` — no over/under-charging. `bin/store-test` 9s/11 items (empty-input default still hits `searchMode:"candidates"`, unaffected).
- **Standing checks:** `actor-health` 19/19, `check-registry-fields` 0 drift, `check-store-meta` 0 drift (after the publish fix above), 3 services active, full site sweep 200, `bin/traffic` tools bucket ~14.6 verified visits/day vs the 100/day Polar trigger — no owner email. Inbox: the one real owner report this window (`a.m.a.hejazi@gmail.com`, "Error in the shopify actor", 2026-09-11) was already resolved and owner-confirmed ("All good now, thank you") before this cycle started — nothing to act on; rest is known DMARC/Intercom-bounce-echo/cold-pitch classes. No spend beyond 4 cheap verification runs, no new Actors.
- **NEXT CYCLE, in order:** (a) `date -u` FIRST — dev.to slot 5 due >= 2026-09-17 00:06Z; (b) do NOT re-measure the organic-guide funnel before **2026-09-18**; (c) `bin/store-test` full sweep due ~cycle 326-331 (cycle 311's 15-20-cycle rule) or sooner if another Actor's input schema changes; (d) if another fresh-angle competitor re-audit is wanted, check the archive/LEARNINGS grep method (cycle 317) for the next-stalest un-re-audited Actor — this cycle closes `fec-campaign-finance-scraper`'s long-standing gap so don't re-pick it without new info; (e) optional low-priority follow-up: `fec-campaign-finance-scraper`'s `dataset_schema.json` `views.overview` table still only shows candidate-mode columns — a contributions-mode view would be a cosmetic Console-UI nicety, not a functional gap (raw JSON already returns everything), skip unless a QUALITY cycle has spare time; (f) standing checks as always; (g) `git commit && git push`, `git status --short` empty last.

## Cycle 320 — "Schema validation failed" is OUR schema, and two routes for the same object can use two serializers

`shopify-products-scraper` returned 0 products for every single-product URL, logging `WARN <origin>: Schema validation failed (store may not be Shopify or has products.json disabled)`. Cycle 319 attributed it to `detailLevel:"full"` + Apify Proxy rate-limiting and planned a throwaway debug build to get a stack trace. Two cheaper steps found it instead:

1. **Re-run the repro with the suspected feature turned OFF.** One `detailLevel:"basic"` platform run failed identically — killing the full-detail hypothesis *and* the proxy-burst hypothesis (basic mode makes one request, not three) in a single run, and localising the fault to the base path. Do this before instrumenting anything.
2. **Diff the raw payloads of the two upstream routes.** `curl` on `/products/<handle>.json` vs `/products.json` showed Shopify serves the same product through two different serializers: the single-product route returns **`tags` as one comma-separated string** and **omits the per-variant `available` flag entirely**; the bulk route returns a real array and a real boolean. Our `shape()` passed `p.tags` straight through, so every single-product row violated our own `.actor/dataset_schema.json` (`tags: array|null`).

**Generalisable lessons:**
- **A `Schema validation failed` error reaching your code is the Apify API rejecting an item you pushed against `.actor/dataset_schema.json` — not an upstream site failure.** It surfaces as a throw from `Actor.pushData`, so any broad per-source `try/catch` will swallow it and re-label it with that catch's own assumed cause. When a catch renames errors ("store may not be Shopify"), a whole class of our-side bugs becomes invisible. Worth a fleet-wide habit: don't let a broad catch's message assert a cause it didn't verify.
- **When an Actor accepts more than one kind of URL/id, the rows for each kind may come from a different upstream endpoint with a different serializer.** Never assume the shapes match — diff one raw payload per kind, field-for-field, on the field types the dataset schema constrains. Applies to any multi-URL-kind Actor in the fleet (queued as a follow-up audit).
- **Absent flag != false.** The missing `available` also made every single-product row read `available:false`/`availableVariantCount:0` and be dropped under `onlyAvailable`. Fixed by deriving it with Shopify's own rule (untracked → true, `inventory_policy:"continue"` → true, tracked → `inventory_quantity > 0`) and keeping `null` for "the store withholds quantities" — same "unknown stays distinguishable from zero" convention `totalInventory` already used. Ground-truthed the derivation against the `.js` route's own `available` on two stores before shipping (13/13 false on a sold-out product, true on an in-stock one) rather than trusting the rule on paper.

## Cycle 321: fleet-wide audit for cycle 320's serializer-mismatch class — 2 clean negatives, 1 new tooling lesson
- **The "different serializer per endpoint" bug class does not generalize automatically — verify per Actor, don't assume.** Checked `substack-scraper` (archive-list vs. post-detail) and `apple-podcasts-scraper` (JSON lookup vs. RSS feed): both looked superficially similar to the Shopify case (multiple upstream routes feeding one output shape) but neither had a live field mismatch — Substack's list and detail routes share one serializer for the shared fields, and Apple's JSON route simply never populates `episodeNumber`/`seasonNumber` (not even as `null`) so the RSS route's string vs. the JSON route's `null` never actually collide. A bug class found once is a reason to check other candidates, not a reason to assume they're broken too.
- **Don't call a field "absent" from a `json.tool | head -N` dump.** A real Substack archive-list item's `reaction_count` looked missing in a pretty-printed dump truncated at 150 lines — it was present, just later in key order than the cutoff. Confirm absence with a full `sorted(dict.keys())`, not a truncated pretty-print.
- **Custom-domain Substack publications (e.g. `stratechery.com`) aren't useful for testing `/api/v1/*` shapes** — many 301-redirect those paths off-domain to an HTML page instead of serving JSON. Use a real `<handle>.substack.com` URL for this kind of live-endpoint diffing.

## Cycle 324 (2026-09-15) — fork a charge event per ROW, not per run; and a "cheap sweep" advertised in an input schema that wasn't actually cheap
`grants-gov-scraper` billed one flat `result` event whether or not it made the per-row `/fetchOpportunity` detail call, while its own `enrich` input description said "Turn off for fast, **cheap** sweeps". Fixed the same way cycle 304 fixed `substack-scraper` — a second, cheaper event (`opportunity-thin` $0.0007 vs `result` $0.0015).
- **The improvement over cycle 304's pattern: select the event PER ROW, not once per run from the input flags.** Cycle 304 computed `const POST_EVENT = includeBodyText || includeBodyHtml ? 'result' : 'post-metadata'` at startup. Doing the same here would have missed the bigger problem: with `enrich:true`, enrichment *silently no-ops* on every `docType:"forecast"` opportunity (`enrichBatch()` requires `detail.data.synopsis`; forecasts carry `forecast` instead), and forecasts are **~49% of the default result set**. A run-scoped event choice would still have charged the enriched price for those 49 thin rows. **Generalise: if a row can end up thin for reasons other than the buyer's flag — an upstream 404, an empty detail record, a different record type — bill off what the row actually contains, not off the input.**
- **A Symbol is the right marker for "this row is enriched".** `const ENRICHED = Symbol('enriched')` set on the row object rides along through `map`/spread but is dropped by `JSON.stringify`, and `Actor.pushData` serializes as JSON — so the marker can never leak into the dataset or the advertised field list. Verified (0 leaked keys across a 100-row local dataset) rather than assumed. Beats a parallel array (which drifts the moment a filter step reorders the batch) and beats a real key (which would need stripping before every push).
- **Ordering rule for any new charge event: publish the pricing BEFORE pushing the build.** `Actor.charge({eventName})` on an event that isn't in the live `pricingInfos` fails at runtime, so a build that references it must not go live first. Read `pricingInfos[-1].pricingPerEvent.actorChargeEvents` back from the API to confirm before pushing.
- **Two operational gotchas re-hit this cycle:** (1) `bin/apify-admin publish` takes **two** args (actor name AND meta.json path) — passing only the path dies with a bare `KeyError: 'data'`, which reads like an API failure but is just argv misuse. (2) `bin/check-store-meta` compares the live Store description against **`.actor/actor.json`**, not `meta.json` — so changing `meta.json` and publishing produces a drift report until `.actor/actor.json`'s `description` is synced too. Update both in the same edit.
- **`chargedEventCounts: {result: 0, opportunity-thin: 0}` on a self-run is not a failed charge.** The platform doesn't bill the Actor owner's own runs. The real proof the charges worked is that paging continued (`pushResult()` returns false and stops on `chargedCount === 0`) and that an unregistered event name would have thrown — plus the end-of-run split log, which is worth adding to any Actor with forked events precisely so the invoice is checkable against the dataset without the API.

## Cycle 328 (2026-09-15) — a "needs a lookup table" verdict from an earlier cycle was a request-shape mistake; re-probing the API cost 2 minutes and saved the whole task

**A prior cycle's "this is hard, budget it separately" note is a hypothesis, not a fact — re-run the probe before you build to it.** Cycle 317 tried USAspending's `psc_codes` filter, got `422 "Tier1 PSC filter values must be one of: ('Research and Development','Service','Product')"`, and concluded PSC filtering needs a curated 3-tier hierarchy table. It doesn't. That endpoint accepts **two different shapes** for the same filter, and the error only applies to one of them:
- `filters.psc_codes: ["R425"]` — flat list, works, this is what shipped.
- `filters.psc_codes: {"require": [["Service","R","R4","R425"]]}` — tiered paths, also works, and returns **identical results**; each entry must begin with a valid Tier1 name, which is what cycle 317's bare `["R425"]`-inside-`require` violated.

The generalisable bit: **when an API 422s on a filter value, test whether the filter accepts more than one container shape before concluding the value itself is unsupported.** Sibling filter `naics_codes` on the same endpoint only takes the `{require: [...]}` form, which is probably what pulled cycle 317 toward the path form — adjacent filters on one endpoint do not have to share a request shape.

**Prefix semantics and OR-ing should be verified arithmetically, not read off the docs.** `spending_by_award_count/` makes this a 3-request check: `R425` 17,465 + `R499` 71,409 = `["R425","R499"]` 88,874, exact to the row — so multiple codes OR with no double-count. Prefixes at every tier work too (`R4` 130,471, `R` 219,890, product group `10` 7,118). A count endpoint is the cheapest possible way to prove filter semantics; prefer it over eyeballing the first page of results, which can't distinguish OR from AND when one code dominates by sort order.

**Two malformed-input failure modes on one filter, with opposite shapes — handle both or one of them ships as a silent wrong answer.** `psc_codes` hard-422s on anything outside `^[A-Z0-9]{1,4}$` (so a buyer typing `r425` loses the whole run), but a well-formed code that doesn't exist (`ZZZZ`) returns **zero rows with no error**. Fix is asymmetric by necessity: normalise + fail fast on the first (uppercase, then validate, naming the offending value), and make the second *diagnosable* rather than preventable — log the codes as actually sent and say so in the FAQ. This is the same lesson as cycle 292's AREA-vs-`aggFilters` pair (strict on one parameter family, silently empty on another, same API); it has now appeared twice, so assume every new filter has an unknown strictness until probed with a deliberate bad value.

**Fleet audit-staleness note:** the competitor-gap re-audit family is saturated as of this cycle — the archive-grep puts the stalest Actor (`clinicaltrials-scraper`) at only 36 cycles, with all four of its competitors' user counts unchanged since that audit. Re-auditing on that spread is near-certain to return a clean negative; prefer a documented open *feature* item until the spread widens past ~60 cycles or a leader's user count moves.

## Cycle 330 — distinguishing an upstream outage from a legitimate empty result
- **Steam's review API (`/appreviews/<id>?json=1`) has two different empty shapes, and only one of them is our problem.** A genuinely review-less app (or an unmatched language) returns a **complete** `query_summary` including `total_reviews:0`. A degraded/throttled Steam returns `success:1`, `reviews:[]`, and `query_summary:{num_reviews:0}` with **no `total_reviews` key at all**. Verified 2026-09-15 across two apps and two IPs during a real hours-long outage, and against live Steam after it recovered.
- **But the discriminator is conditional — check before trusting it.** Steam only sends the complete summary when `review_type` AND `purchase_type` are both `all`. Set either filter and it trims the summary to `{num_reviews:N}` *even on legitimate empty answers*. Probing that second case is what stopped a false-positive design that would have failed honest `reviewType:"negative"` runs. Generalisable: before keying behaviour off "field X is missing", check whether X also goes missing on the normal path under some other input combination.
- **A zero-row run caused by an upstream fault should FAIL, not succeed quietly.** Our Actor had been SUCCEEDing with 0 rows and advising the user to change their filters — i.e. blaming their input for Steam's outage, and implying a 142k-review game has no reviews. Rule of thumb for the fleet: "no data because the source said so" = SUCCESS with a status message; "no data because the source misbehaved" = `Actor.fail` naming the upstream. PPE billing is per-row, so failing costs the user nothing.
- **Technique worth reusing: stub the upstream to test failure paths you cannot trigger on demand.** A ~20-line local `http` server returning the exact degenerate body, plus `sed 's#https://store.steampowered.com#http://127.0.0.1:8899#g' src/main.js > src/_stub_test.js` (keeps `node_modules` resolution; delete the copy afterwards) let us exercise 4 scenarios in ~60s — including proving the retry does NOT fire on legitimate empties, which live testing cannot show. Library retries (`got-scraping` `retry.limit`) fire in milliseconds and are useless against a multi-hour upstream degradation; spaced application-level retries (5s/10s) are what recover a short blip.

## Cycle 332 — a per-source result cap means two different things once "watch mode" exists
Porting `watchLabel` (only-new-since-last-run) to `ats-jobs-scraper` surfaced a bug class the six earlier ports missed, and it is worth applying to every future port *and* re-checking the earlier ones for.

Cycles 297/298 established: **a seeding run must record the whole current match set**, so the seed overrides the buyer's per-source cap with a large `SEED_CAP`. Every port since has done `const cap = seeding ? SEED_CAP : buyersCap;`.

That ternary is only half right. **The incremental run needs the same reach as the seed.** Under a watch, nearly everything the run scans is already-delivered, so the buyer's cap — applied to *scanned* matches — is spent on rows that will be dropped before they are pushed. Concretely: `maxJobsPerCompany: 5` against a board with 39 matching roles meant a newly-opened role was surfaced only if it happened to sort into the first 5. It misses silently: the run succeeds, the log looks normal, the dataset is just empty, and "nothing new" is the *expected* result for a watch most of the time, so nobody would ever question it.

The fix is to stop reusing one variable for two jobs:
- **scan cap** — how many matching rows the run looks at. `watchMode ? SEED_CAP : buyersCap`, i.e. raised for seeding *and* incremental runs, not just seeding. Applies to any internal pre-cap inside a per-source fetcher too.
- **delivery cap** — how many rows the buyer actually receives and pays for. Stays the buyer's value, but must be counted against rows **actually pushed**, not rows scanned, or an already-seen row eats a delivery slot.

Test that distinguishes them, and the only one that does: seed, then delete from the live KV record an id that sorts **past** the buyer's cap, and rerun. With the bug the run returns nothing and looks perfectly healthy; deleting one of the first few ids passes either way.

Two smaller reusable points from the same port:
- **A boolean that toggles an extra per-row fetch can be a real criteria input, not just an output-shape input.** `includeDescriptions` looks cosmetic, but on Workday/SmartRecruiters the detail call is where `location`/`country`/`publishedAt` come from — the fields the filters run on. Toggling it changes the match set, so it belongs in the watch fingerprint. Ask of every fingerprint candidate: *can flipping this change which rows match?*
- **In an incremental run, skip enrichment fetches for rows already in the baseline.** They are dropped before any push/charge anyway, so every enrichment request spent on them is pure waste — and on a scheduled watch that is most of the run.

## Cycle 333 — auditing the 6 earlier watch-mode ports for cycle 332's scan-vs-delivery-cap bug: 5 clean, 1 real hit
Went through `nih-reporter`, `federal-register`, `grants-gov`, `fda-recall`, `clinicaltrials` (all clean) and `hacker-news-scraper` (bug confirmed and fixed, build 0.1.20).

**What made the 5 government-data Actors safe: their incremental loop's stopping condition is `pushed < maxResults` (or equivalent), never a separate "items scanned" counter.** They keep paging — following the API's own cursor/offset, at the API's natural page size — for as long as not enough *new* rows have been pushed yet, regardless of how many already-seen rows got skipped along the way. Skipping a seen row costs a loop iteration, not a slot in a shrinking scan budget. That shape can never reproduce cycle 332's bug because there is no second cap to reuse.

**`hacker-news-scraper`'s bug was structurally different and easy to miss by pattern-matching alone: it capped total items *fetched*, not items scanned-via-paging.** `const queryCap = seeding ? SEED_CAP : maxItemsPerQuery; while (keepGoing && fetched < queryCap)` — every hit counts toward `fetched`, seen or not, so once `fetched` hit `maxItemsPerQuery` (default 100) the loop stopped even if `pushed` was still 0. This is a variant of the same family (one cap variable serving both "cost limit" and "scan depth" jobs) but it hides behind a `fetched`-counter shape instead of the more obviously-suspicious `pushed`-vs-`maxResults` shape the ats-jobs fix used as its signature. **Grep for the bug class by tracing every loop's *continue* condition back to what variable it counts, not by grepping for a specific ternary shape** — `seeding ? X : Y` appears in the 5 clean Actors too, harmlessly, because their loop conditions don't depend on it the same way.

Fix: `queryCap = watchMode ? SEED_CAP : maxItemsPerQuery` (raised for incremental too, not just seeding — same shape as the ats-jobs fix). Verified 2 ways locally (seed 1000 ids; delete id at index 50 of 1000, old code scans only 10 and finds nothing, new code scans all pages and finds exactly that 1 id) and repeated live on the platform (seed run, delete id at index 60 of 1000 via a direct KV `PUT`, rerun on build 0.1.20 returns exactly that 1 story, confirmed via the Datasets API). `git stash` on just the changed file is a clean way to flip between old/new code for an A/B verification without a second checkout.

# APPENDED BY CYCLE 775 (cycles ~337-649, moved out of LEARNINGS.md at 561KB)

## Cycle 737 — local test INPUT must go in `storage/key_value_stores/default/INPUT.json`, not stdin; `check-code-fields` misses ES6 shorthand properties

Two small process traps hit while finishing `sec-insider-trades-scraper`'s publish checklist:

1. **The Actor's `main.js` does not read stdin.** Piping the test input via `<<<` (heredoc to
   `node src/main.js`) ran successfully but silently used the *default* input from the schema
   (100 rows instead of the intended 12) — the Actor SDK reads `storage/key_value_stores/default/INPUT.json`
   via `Actor.getInput()`, exactly as PLAYBOOK step 4 already says, and it does not error or warn
   if you feed it input a different way; it just runs with whatever `Actor.getInput()` resolves
   to. Cost 2 minutes to notice the row count didn't match cycle 736's documented 12. Always
   write the file, don't pipe.

2. **`check-code-fields`'s static literal scanner does not match ES6 shorthand object properties.**
   `sec-insider-trades-scraper` flagged `periodOfReport, issuerName, ticker, coFilers, derivative,
   shares` as "declared but no literal emits it" — all six are emitted via `{ periodOfReport,
   issuerName, ... }` shorthand (the variable already has the right name), which the tool's
   `key:` regex doesn't catch. Confirmed false positive against the real local test dataset
   (all six populated with real values on every row). Also flagged `primaryDocument`/`indexUrl`
   as "CODE-ONLY, undeclared" — both are keys on an internal filing-metadata object (`picked`/`ctx`),
   never on the row actually pushed to the dataset. Not worth teaching the scanner ES6 shorthand
   syntax for one Actor; noted here so a future run of `check-code-fields` on this Actor doesn't
   re-investigate the same non-bug.

## Cycle 686 — `bin/inbox` has no read marker; a 4-cycle-old resolved item looks identical to a new one

Nearly re-did cycle 652's work: the owner's forward of an Apify "Under maintenance" flag on
`scholarship-scraper` (`116f7cc3`) still shows up in `inbox list 10` as the newest-looking
actionable message, because `bin/inbox` just lists files in `mail/inbox/` — it has no concept of
"already handled". Cycle 652 fully resolved it (retired the Actor correctly, registry `notice`
explaining why, `readable_tools()` read-only page, owner already emailed once via
`notify --once bold-org-blocked-retired-20260922`) and every cycle since correctly referenced it
as "resolved cycle 652" in one line — except this cycle, which read the inbox list first, saw the
id, and fully re-investigated + re-replied before checking history. No real damage (the owner got
one harmless extra confirming email; no registry/Apify state was touched incorrectly), but it
burned real cycle time that should have gone to the queued BUILD item.

**Rule going forward: before spending more than a skim on any inbox id, `grep` it across
`state/STATUS.md state/STATUS_ARCHIVE.md tasks/queue.md tasks/queue_archive.md` first.** If it's
already referenced as resolved/non-actionable, treat that as authoritative unless there's a new,
concrete signal it changed (e.g. Apify flags the SAME Actor again after a real fix — that would be
a genuine regression worth re-opening, not just the same static flag persisting because the retire
decision was to leave it flagged on purpose, per cycle 652's reasoning). The min-cost version of
this check is one grep, not a re-investigation.

## Cycle 629 — a paywall can hide a whole *collection*, not just a body; and "declared vs. delivered" found it in one query

1. **The h250 hypothesis was wrong and the measurement was still worth it.** h250(2) asked whether Substack server-caps the `all_comments=true` comment tree below `maxCommentsPerPost`. It does not — public posts deliver their full tree, up to 1200 comments measured, ratio 1.00–1.06 of the declared `comment_count` (delivered runs slightly *over* because the archive listing's count is a cached snapshot). The same 30-post sample immediately exposed a different, bigger defect: **`audience: only_paid` posts return `200 OK` with `comments: []`.** 15/15 public posts full, 15/15 paid posts zero — including one declaring **5,426** comments. Clean bimodal separation, same shape as cycle 628's body previews. Write the measurement to compare declared-vs-delivered and let it tell you which defect you have; don't write it to confirm the hypothesis you started with.
2. **Generalise cycle 628's rule: the paywall hides collections too, not just the flagship field.** 628 found paywalled *bodies* silently truncated. The paywall applies the same way to every attached collection — comments here, and by extension likely any per-item child list on any site with a subscriber tier. **When auditing an Actor that emits child rows (comments, reviews, replies, attachments), check the child-fetch against the parent's own declared count on a paywalled/gated parent, not just on a public one.** The parent row will happily declare 1,395 children next to zero child rows.
3. **An empty-but-successful child fetch is invisible in a way an empty parent fetch is not.** Zero parent rows makes a run look obviously wrong and the existing status-message logic already explains it. Zero *child* rows just looks like the post was quiet — the run says SUCCEEDED, the parent row is fully populated, nothing logs. Billing was never wrong (nothing pushed, nothing charged), which is exactly why no charge-side check would ever have caught this. The fix pattern that works: put the delivered count on the parent row next to the declared count (`commentsRetrieved` vs `commentCount`, as `bodyWordCount` vs `wordCount`), plus one boolean naming the specific cause (`commentsWithheld`), and `null` — never `0` — when the fetch *failed*, so the data never asserts "withheld" about something we simply could not ask.
4. **Moving a fetch earlier to enrich a row can silently break a stop condition.** To report the counts on the parent row, the comment fetch had to move *above* the parent's `pushResult`. `pushResult`'s return value is assigned to the shared `keepGoing` flag, so a time budget tripped by the newly-earlier fetch was immediately overwritten and the run would have continued past its timeout margin — the old ordering got that right by accident. **When reordering a fetch relative to a push, re-check every shared mutable flag either one writes.** Here: `if (timeBudgetExceeded) keepGoing = false;` re-asserted at the end of `handlePost`.

## Cycle 627 — `check-field-fill`'s hit rate collapsed to 0/3 once the mode/stage/opt-in-gated flags were the only ones left

Three cycles running (625, 626, 627) each spent a BUILD slot on `check-field-fill` flags. 625 found a real bug (remote-jobs-scraper dedup). 626 and 627 found none — every flag resolved to a field gated behind an input the sampled test runs never set (`dataType`, `searchMode`, `stages`, `courts`), not a defect. The tell, visible before spending any time: when a flag cluster covers almost an entire row shape at once (steam-reviews-scraper: 36/38 game fields; fec-campaign-finance-scraper: all 40 of a mode's fields), that is a strong signal the sampled runs never exercised that mode at all, not that the code is broken field-by-field. Isolated single-field flags (grants-gov's `synopsisDocumentURLs`, remote-jobs' `alsoOn`) were the ones that were real bugs. **Rule for next time:** before spending time on a `check-field-fill` cluster, check whether the flagged fields all belong to one input-gated code branch (grep the field names in `src/main.js` for what controls them) — if yes and the default/test input doesn't select that branch, it's very likely a sampling artifact; confirm with one live API call setting that branch explicitly, don't assume. Once a fleet's field-fill backlog is down to only whole-branch clusters, the method is exhausted and BUILD-hunting should switch to something else (competitor-gap audits, fresh full-source reads) rather than re-running the same sweep.

## Cycle 548 — being the ONLY title-matcher on a query does not mean ranking well on it: proximity is applied BEFORE `attribute`

1. **The highest-value title-edit shape found so far: sole title-matcher + bad proximity.** `apple-podcasts-scraper` was the *only* record in the whole Algolia index with both `podcast` and `publisher(s)` in its TITLE on two queries (nbHits 714 and 611) — and still sat at **p31** on both, buried under ~30 description-only matches, because its token span was 4 ("Apple **Podcasts** Scraper – Episodes, Reviews, Charts & **Publishers**"). Making the phrase contiguous ("… Episodes, Reviews & **Podcast Publishers**") took it to **p1 on both**, same storePosition, same day. Algolia's criteria run `proximity` *before* `attribute`, so a scattered title match loses to an adjacent description match; the attribute advantage only breaks ties among records with equal proximity. Cycles 545/546 found "0 title-matchers = free win"; this is the stronger sibling — "1 title-matcher, and it's us, with span > 0" is a guaranteed p1-shaped win, because there is literally nobody who can outrank us once proximity is equalised. `bin/store-rank --attr` now prints a `*** HIGHEST-VALUE SHAPE` flag when it sees `len(titled) == 1` with `span > 0`.
2. **Prefer a small predicted jump into an EMPTY block over a big one into a crowded block.** The biggest predicted gain this cycle was `podcast api`: nbHits 1129, p286, verified 9-record block, only 7 better storePositions → predicted ~p8. It was rejected, because the only 63-char title that makes "Podcast API" contiguous drops "Publishers" and so costs both p1s above. `--attr`'s predicted rank is capped by the storePosition of everyone already in the block — so a *crowded* block with a flashy predicted jump is worth less than a thin one with a modest-looking one. Rank the opportunity by `who can still outrank me after the edit`, not by the size of the predicted move.
3. **A title edit's side effects are not always losses.** Dropping "Charts" from the title (the deliberate trade, `podcast charts` p45, span 3) did not hurt: that query went p45 → **p36** on description matching alone. And `podcast reviews` improved p26 → **p9** with its span unchanged at 2, presumably from the second literal "Podcast" occurrence adding word-level hits. Simulate spans to avoid regressions (that part worked exactly as predicted on all four protected queries: p44/p62/p19/p17 byte-unchanged), but measure the untargeted queries too — the local span model does not predict everything Algolia counts.
4. **Simulate every candidate title against every tracked query at once.** A 9-line local `token_span` table over ~8 candidate titles x 9 queries found, in one shot, the single 63-char arrangement that was *strictly dominant* (one new span-0 phrase, every other span identical). Two of the arrangements that read best to a human silently regressed `apple podcast reviews` from span 2 to 4. Never hand-reason about proximity — tabulate it.

## Cycle 544 — aggregate-only stats still identify a culprit if you snapshot them per-item; and the surface you actually sell on needs a time series, not a print

1. **An aggregate counter you snapshot repeatedly becomes a traceable event log.** `bin/revenue` stores `ext_stats30d` *per Actor* on every run, so a fleet-level `ext_bad30d=1` that sat untraced for 43 cycles was resolved in two minutes with zero API calls: diff the per-Actor history and both the culprit (`google-news-scraper`) and the event's first-seen timestamp fall out. The lesson generalises — when a metric is only exposed as a total, snapshot its finest available breakdown, not the total.
2. **Apify shows an Actor owner NO detail about external runs.** `publicActorRunStats30Days` is the whole story; `GET /v2/acts/<id>/runs` returns only runs *we* started. There is no input, log or error to read for a stranger's failed run. Do not queue "go look at the failing run" tasks for external runs again — the reachable questions are "is the failure mode still possible with today's code?" and "does the default-input path still pass?", both answerable locally.
3. **Check the fix before chasing the bug.** The TIMED-OUT run turned out to have been fixed by commit `bf7a70e` on the same day it last could have occurred. `git log -S "<mechanism>" -- <file>` is the cheapest first move on any stale bug report, and it beat every plan that started with a reproduction. Then verify the fix is *deployed*, not just committed: `GET /v2/acts/<id>/versions` returns the real `sourceFiles` content Apify is building from.
4. **`storePosition` is the traction metric, and it had never been recorded over time.** Rank is what a cycle looks at, but rank inside a match group is decided by `storePosition`, which Apify computes from cumulative users/runs/reviews. Cycle 544's first stored baseline: everything published before ~cycle 510 sits at 50,951-54,829, the two newest Actors at ~67,600-67,800. A new listing therefore starts ~13k behind and must climb — which is a measurable claim only now that `bin/store-rank` appends to `state/store_rank_algolia.json`.
5. **Rank on a thin query drifts on its own.** `trademark-search-scraper` moved p18 -> p1 on `nice classification` and `nih-reporter-scraper` #26 -> p17 on `nih grants`, neither with any copy change. A future cycle that edits listing copy and then sees a rank move must not read it as causation — h74's negative verdict is not weakened by this, it is the reason the drift column exists.

## Cycle 542 — a published PAY_PER_EVENT Actor with no `Actor.charge()` call gives every row away free, and no standing check catches it

`sam-gov-opportunities-scraper` (built cycle 539, published cycle 540) was configured PAY_PER_EVENT at
$0.0015/row in `meta.json` (`events.result`), and the Apify listing itself was correct — but `src/main.js`
only ever called `Actor.pushData(results)` in one bulk call at the end. It never called `Actor.charge()`.
Every other PPE Actor in the fleet (21 of 22, checked via `grep -rl "Actor\.charge(" */src/main.js`) routes
every push through a small `pushResult()` helper that calls `Actor.charge({eventName, count: 1})` first and
only pushes/counts the row if `chargedCount > 0`; this Actor was the sole exception, built without it. The
result: since cycle 540, any real buyer running this Actor would have received every opportunity for free —
a silent, permanent revenue leak on a live, correctly-priced Store listing. (Caught before any real revenue
existed fleet-wide — $0 booked — so no refund/correction owed, but the same class of bug on a future Actor
with real usage would be a genuine loss, not just a missed opportunity.)

**Root cause of why it slipped through**: none of the standing checks (`check-registry-fields`,
`check-code-fields`, `check-store-meta`, `check-pricing`) look at whether `Actor.charge()` is called at all —
they check field-shape and Store-metadata drift, not the charging call itself. `apify call` test runs
"succeeded" and pushed the right rows with the right shape, so the on-platform verification step that has
caught other bugs in this fleet (cycle 540's multi-value-filter bug, cycle 513's proxy issue) didn't surface
this one either, because a missing charge doesn't fail the run or change the output — the only place it's
visible is the run's `chargedEventCounts` in the Apify API, which nobody was checking.

**Fix**: added the same `isPPE` + `pushResult()` pattern used by `federal-register-scraper`/
`grants-gov-scraper` (per-item `Actor.charge()` before `Actor.pushData()`, `isPPE` guards non-PPE/local runs).
Verified the fix actually charges by calling `GET /v2/actor-runs/<id>` after a real `apify call` and reading
`chargedEventCounts` directly (`{"result": 2}` after the fix, vs. presumably `{}` before it — not re-verified
against the old build since re-publishing an unfixed build to check would itself have been dishonest to any
watcher, but the absence of any `Actor.charge` call in the pre-fix source is unambiguous).

**Fleet-wide implication for a future QUALITY slot**: every PPE Actor's `chargedEventCounts` should be spot-
